4.2.3.
Properties

The contraction and split decomposition operations are inverse of one another in the sense that, with the notation of
Definitions

4.5
and
4.3
,

(15)

T\xrightarrow[\kappa\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi]{\chi}T^{\prime}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(s^{*},t^{*})}T,\qquad\text{and}\qquad T\xrightarrow{(s^{*},t^{*})}T^{\prime}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T^{\prime}\xrightarrow[\chi_{v}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{v^{\prime}}]{\chi_{new}}T.

We next show that performing an edge contraction or a vertex split on a chirotope tree does not change its associated chirotope.

Proposition 4.7
.

If
T\xrightarrow{e}T^{\prime}
or
T\xrightarrow[\kappa\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi]{\chi}T^{\prime}
, then
\chi_{T}=\chi_{T^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We first prove the statement under the hypothesis that
T\xrightarrow{e}T^{\prime}
. We proceed by induction on the number of nodes in
T
. If
T

has two nodes, then the result follows from
Lemma

4.1
.
Assume the result holds for all chirotope trees with
n\geq 2
nodes,
and let
T
be a chirotope trees with
n+1
nodes, let
e
be an edge
of
T
, and and let
T^{\prime}
be such that
T\xrightarrow{e}T^{\prime}
. Since
T
has
at least three nodes, there is an edge
f\neq e
in
T
, and that
edge also exist in
T^{\prime}
. Removing
f
(from
T
) splits
T
into two
parts
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, and removing
f
(from
T^{\prime}
) splits
T^{\prime}
into
two parts
T^{\prime}_{1}
and
T^{\prime}_{2}
; without loss of generality, we assume
that
e
belongs to
T_{1}
and
T^{\prime}_{1}
, so that
T^{\prime}_{2}=T_{2}
and
T_{1}\xrightarrow{e}T^{\prime}_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\chi_{T_{1}}=\chi_{T^{\prime}_{1}}
. Also applying
Lemma

4.1
twice yields

\chi_{T}=\chi_{T_{1}}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{T_{2}},\qquad\chi_{T^{\prime}}=\chi_{T^{\prime}_{1}}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s_{1}}}{{}_{s_{2}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{T^{\prime}_{2}},

where
f=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
. Altogether this implies
\chi_{T}=\chi_{T^{\prime}}
, and the lemma is proved under the hypothesis that
T\xrightarrow{e}T^{\prime}
.

If
T\xrightarrow[\kappa\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{t^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi]{\chi}T^{\prime}
, then
Eq.

15
and the first statement proved in this proposition also ensures that
\chi_{T}=\chi_{T^{\prime}}
, concluding the proof.
∎

4.3.
Canonical chirotope trees

We recall from the introduction that a chirotope tree is called
canonical

if every node is decorated by a
convex or indecomposable chirotope, and if no edge connects two
nodes decorated by convex chirotopes.
The goal of the section is to prove
Theorem

1.3
,
which we copy here for convenience.

Theorem

1.3
.

For every chirotope
\chi
, there exists a unique canonical
chirotope tree
T
such that
\chi_{T}=\chi
.

Suppose that we start with the trivial chirotope tree with a single
node decorated with
\chi
,
T^{tr}_{\chi}
, and, for as long as we
can and in any order, we split the nodes decorated by decomposable
nonconvex chirotopes, and contract the edges joining nodes decorated
by convex chirotopes. The gist of the proof of

Theorem

1.3
is to prove that (i) any such
sequence of operations terminates, (ii) any canonical tree
T
with

\chi_{T}=\chi
can be obtained by such a sequence of operations, and
(iii) all sequences of operations produce the same canonical tree. A
convenient setting to carry out this analysis is the theory of rewriting
systems.

4.3.1.
The rewriting system

Using the contraction and split operations, we define a rewriting system on
chirotope trees.

Definition 4.8
.

We consider the set of chirotope trees equipped with the following rewriting rules:

•

T\xrightarrow{\lozenge}T^{\prime}
if
T^{\prime}
is obtained from
T
by contracting an edge of
T
between two
convex
chirotopes,

•

T\xrightarrow{\pap}T^{\prime}
if
T^{\prime}
is obtained from
T
by splitting a
nonconvex
node of
T
,

We write
T\Rightarrow T^{\prime}
if
T\xrightarrow{\lozenge}T^{\prime}
or
T\xrightarrow{\pap}T^{\prime}
.

As usual, for any rewriting rule
\to
, we denote by
\to^{*}
the
rewriting rule which consists in any number (possibly zero) of
successive applications of
\to
. By definition, a chirotope tree is
canonical if the rewriting rule
\Rightarrow
does not apply to it.

4.3.2.
Termination

The existence of a canonical chirotope tree representing any given
chirotope
\chi
follows from the termination property of

\Rightarrow
. The proof of that property uses the notion of multiset
ordering.
Given two finite multisets
M
and
N
of positive
integers, we write
M>_{mul}N
if there exist multisets
X\subseteq M
and
Y
such that
N=(M\setminus X)\uplus Y
and

\max(X)>\max(Y)
. It is standard that
>_{mul}
defines a
well-founded order (i.e. without infinite decreasing subsequences) on
mutisets of
\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Every rewriting sequence
T_{0}\Rightarrow T_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow T_{n}\Rightarrow\ldots
is finite.

Proof.

Let us associate with a chirotope tree
T
a pair
(N(T),C(T))
,
where
N(T)
is the multiset of sizes of nonconvex chirotopes in
T
,
and
C(T)
is the number of convex chirotopes in
T
.
Suppose that
T\Rightarrow T^{\prime}
.
If
T\xrightarrow{\lozenge}T^{\prime}
, then
C(T^{\prime})=C(T)-1
and
N(T^{\prime})=N(T)
; indeed,
by
Corollary

3.4
, we replace two nodes decorated by convex chirotopes, by a single one,
also decorated by a convex chirotope. If
T\xrightarrow{\pap}T^{\prime}
, the split operation replaces one node with a nonconvex decoration
by two new nodes with decorations of smaller sizes. Thus we have
N(T)>_{mul}N(T^{\prime})
(in this case,
C(T)

can change as one of the new node can have a convex decoration).
In both cases, we have
(N(T^{\prime}),C(T^{\prime}))<_{lex}(N(T),C(T))
,
where
<_{lex}
denotes the lexicographic order corresponding to the pair of orders
(<_{mul},<_{\mathbb{N}})
.
This lexicographic order is well-founded (since both original orders are well-founded),
implying that applying the rewriting rule
\Rightarrow
always terminates.
∎

4.3.3.
Accessibility

The next step is to show that the rewriting rule can turn
T^{tr}_{\chi}
into any canonical chirotope tree whose associated chirotope is
\chi
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
T
be a canonical chirotope tree with
\chi_{T}=\chi
. Then
T^{tr}_{\chi}\,\Rightarrow^{*}\,T
.

Proof.

We start from
T
and contract edges one by one, until we have a
tree with a single node. Since contractions do not modify the
associated chirotope, we end up with

T^{tr}_{\chi}
.
Corollary

3.4
asserts that the node
obtained by an edge contraction is decorated by a convex chirotope
(if and) only if the two merged nodes are decorated by convex
chirotopes. Since the tree we start with is canonical, an immediate
induction yields that none of the trees appearing in this sequence
of contractions has an edge joining two nodes decorated by convex
chirotopes. Each contraction we performed is therefore the inverse of a
split operation allowed in
\Rightarrow
, proving the claim.
∎

4.3.4.
Confluence

It remains to show that, starting from
T^{tr}_{\chi}
, independently of
the choice of rewriting operations, iterating
\Rightarrow
as much as
possible always yields the same output. This property is known as

confluence
in the theory of rewriting rules. It is well-known
(Newman’s lemma) that, for a terminating rewriting rule (like
\Rightarrow
, see
Lemma

4.9
), confluence is implied by the following
local confluence
property.

Proposition 4.11
.

If
T,T_{1},T_{2}
are three chirotope trees such that
T\Rightarrow T_{1}
and
T\Rightarrow T_{2}
, then there exists a chirotope tree
T^{\prime}
such that
T_{1}\Rightarrow^{*}T^{\prime}
and
T_{2}\Rightarrow^{*}T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The statement is immediate if the transformations
T\Rightarrow T_{1}
and
T\Rightarrow T_{2}
operate on disjoint sets of nodes:
indeed, each operation can be performed after the other one, and the
resulting chirotope tree does not depend on the order of
operations. If the transformations operate on nondisjoint sets of
nodes then they must be of the same type as
\xrightarrow{\lozenge}

operates on two nodes decorated by convex chirotopes whereas any of
the nodes on which
\xrightarrow{\pap}
operates is decorated by a
nonconvex chirotope. If
T\xrightarrow{\lozenge}T_{1}
and

T\xrightarrow{\lozenge}T_{2}
, the local confluence follows from the
associativity of
\pap
. So let us consider we are in the last case, with

T\xrightarrow[\kappa\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi]{\chi}T_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad T\xrightarrow[\alpha\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\beta]{\chi}T_{2},

and
\chi
a nonconvex chirotope decorating a node
v
of
T
. The
analysis turns out to be considerably more intricate.

Let
G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}X\uplus S
denote the ground set of
\chi
, with
X
denoting the nonproxy elements of the ground set, and
S
its proxy elements.
Throughout the analysis, we only make modifications in the tree that are local to the node labeled by
\chi
.
Accordingly we will only represent in pictures the node decorated with
\chi
and the nodes replacing it after various operations.
For example
T
,
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are visualized as follows:

.

Let
\{Y,Z\}
be the modular bipartition of
X\cup S
associated to
the bowtie decomposition
\chi=\kappa\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
, and

\{A,B\}
be the modular bipartition of
X\cup S
associated with

\chi=\alpha\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{a^{*}}}{{}_{b^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\beta
. We assume that
\{A,B\}\neq\{Y,Z\}
as otherwise
T_{1}=T_{2}
and the statement trivially
follows. Since
\chi
is nonconvex,
Corollary

3.4

ensures that
\alpha
or
\beta
is nonconvex, and the same goes for

\kappa
or
\xi
.

Let us first asssume that
A\subsetneq Y
, and therefore
Z\subsetneq B
. If
\kappa
is convex, then
\alpha
is convex too, hence

\beta
is not convex. Therefore, up to exchanging the role of

(A,B)
and
(Y,Z)
, we can assume that
A\subsetneq Y
and that

\kappa
is not convex.

From
A\subsetneq Y
, it comes that the pair
\{A,\{y^{*}\}\cup Y\setminus A\}
is then a non trivial modular bipartition of

\kappa
, as
\chi
and
\kappa
coincide on
Y
, and
y^{*}
is a
proxy representing the elements of
Z
.
Let
T_{3}
be the split of
T_{1}
according to the corresponding bowtie
decomposition
\kappa=\kappa_{1}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\kappa_{2}
.
Since
\kappa
is not convex, we have
T_{1}\Rightarrow T_{3}
. The tree

T_{3}
is visualized as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{T3}\end{array}.

By associativity (
Proposition

3.11
), we have

\chi=(\kappa_{1}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\kappa_{2})\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi=\kappa_{1}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{c^{*}}}{{}_{d^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}(\kappa_{2}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi)
. This means
that
\kappa_{1}
(resp.
\kappa_{2}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
) is the chirotope
associated to the module
A
(resp.
B
) in the corresponding bowtie
decomposition. Hence, we have
\kappa_{1}=\alpha
and

(\kappa_{2}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi)=\beta
(up to renaming the proxy
elements
c^{*}
in
a^{*}
and
d^{*}
in
b^{*}
). This implies

T_{2}\xrightarrow[\kappa_{2}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y^{*}}}{{}_{z^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi]{\beta}T_{3}.

If
\beta
is not convex, then
T_{2}\Rightarrow T_{3}
and the
statement follows with
T^{\prime}=T_{3}
. If
\beta
is convex, then both

\kappa_{2}
and
\xi
are, and
T_{3}\Rightarrow T_{2}
, proving the
statement with
T^{\prime}=T_{2}
.

A schematic representation of the argument is presented on
Figure

6
.

Figure 6.
Diamond diagram proving local confluence in the case
A\subsetneq Y
.

The cases
A\subsetneq Z
,
B\subsetneq Y
,
B\subsetneq Z
are
similar to the case
A\subsetneq Y
, so from now on we can assume
that
A\cap Y
,
A\cap Z
,
B\cap Y
,
B\cap Z
are all nonempty.

Lemma

3.9
then implies that each of
A\cap Y
,

A\cap Z
,
B\cap Y
and
B\cap Z
is a nontrivial module of

\chi
. We use this to further decompose
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in ways that
eventually reduce to the same term. The specific decomposition
depends on which of
A\cap Y
,
A\cap Z
,
B\cap Y
,
B\cap Z

contain nonextremal elements. We spell out two representative cases
and leave the remaining, routine, check to the reader.

Case 1: each of
A\cap Y
,
A\cap Z
,
B\cap Y
,
B\cap Z
contains
at least one nonextremal element.

Many different chirotopes now enter the stage, so we record their
ground sets and proxies in tables to help the reader keep track of
them.

The ground set of
\kappa
is
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}=(A\cap Y)\cup(B\cap Y)\cup\{y^{*}\}
and
\chi
and
\kappa
coincide on
Y
. It
follows that
A\cap Y
and
B\cap Y
are disjoint nontrivial
modules of
\kappa
, and
\kappa
therefore decomposes into
\kappa=\sigma\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{x_{AY}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{x_{AY}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{x_{AY}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{s^{*}}}{{}_{x_{AY}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\kappa^{\triangle}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x_{BY}}}{{}_{p^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x_{BY}}}{{}_{p^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x_{BY}}}{{}_{p^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x_{BY}}}{{}_{p^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\pi
.

Chirotope

Ground set

Proxies

\kappa

Y\cup\{y^{*}\}

y^{*}

\sigma

(A\cap Y)\cup\{s^{*}\}

s^{*}

\pi

(B\cap Y)\cup\{p^{*}\}

p^{*}

\kappa^{\triangle}

\{x_{AY},x_{BY},y^{*}\}

x_{AY},x_{BY},y^{*}

Chirotope

Ground set

Proxies

\xi

Z\cup\{z^{*}\}

z^{*}

\tau

(B\cap Z)\cup\{t^{*}\}

t^{*}

\rho

(A\cap Z)\cup\{r^{*}\}

r^{*}

\xi^{\triangle}

\{x_{AZ},x_{BZ},z^{*}\}

x_{AZ},x_{BZ},z^{*}

Similarly, the ground set of
\xi
is
Z\cup\{z^{*}\}=(A\cap Z)\cup(B\cap Z)\cup\{z^{*}\}
and
\chi
and
\xi
coincide on

Z
. It follows that
A\cap Z
and
B\cap Z
are disjoint
nontrivial modules of
\xi
, and
\xi
therefore decomposes into

\xi=\tau\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{t^{*}}}{{}_{x_{BZ}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{t^{*}}}{{}_{x_{BZ}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{t^{*}}}{{}_{x_{BZ}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{t^{*}}}{{}_{x_{BZ}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi^{\triangle}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x_{AZ}}}{{}_{r^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x_{AZ}}}{{}_{r^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x_{AZ}}}{{}_{r^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x_{AZ}}}{{}_{r^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\rho
.

Altogether, we can transform
T_{1}
into

where the last transformation is a contraction of the central edge
between the two convex chirotopes
\kappa^{\Delta}
and
\xi^{\Delta}
;
the resulting node is decorated with a convex chirotopes on four
elements, all proxies; we denote this chirotope by

\chi^{\square}
, and its proxy elements are
x_{AY},x_{AZ},x_{BZ},x_{BY}
. Similarly, we have

We stress that the chirotopes
\sigma,\rho,\tau,\pi
and

\chi^{\square}
are the same in both decompositions, as they are
restriction of our initial chirotope
\chi
to the same sets
(replacing proxy elements by arbitrary elements in the sets that
they represent). The two trees obtained at the far right of both
derivations are identical. Since each of
A\cap Y
,
A\cap Z
,

B\cap Y
and
B\cap Z
contains a nonextremal point, the
chirotopes
\sigma,\rho,\tau
and
\pi
are non convex, and all
transformations in the decompositions of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
given
above are valid rules for
\Rightarrow
, and the statement follows.

showing the local confluence in this case. Other cases are treated similarly.
∎

4.3.5.
Concluding the proof of
Theorem

1.3

We can now put things together to prove existence and uniqueness of the canonical chirotope tree representing any given chirotope
\chi
.

Let
\chi
be a chirotope. As noticed in
Section

4.3.2
,
the existence of a canonical chirotope tree representing
\chi
is a consequence of
Lemma

4.9
.
Moreover, by
Lemma

4.10
any canonical chirotope tree representing
\chi
can be obtained from
T^{tr}_{\chi}

through the rewriting rule
\Rightarrow
. We also observe that we can never apply any further rewriting
\Rightarrow
to a canonical
chirotope tree, i.e. these are terminal states.
But we have proved the confluence of the rewriting rule
\Rightarrow
(
Proposition

4.11
, combined with Newman’s lemma),
so that for a given initial state, there is a unique possible terminal state.
Consequenly, there is a unique canonical chirotope tree representing
\chi
, and
Theorem

1.3
is proved. ∎

5.
Almost all realizable chirotopes are indecomposable

In this section, we consider only realizable chirotopes.
Recall from the introduction that
t_{n}
and
d_{n}
are respectively the number of
realizable chirotopes and decomposable realizable chirotopes on
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Our goal is to prove
Theorem

1.5
,
which we copy here for convenience.

Theorem

1.5
.

For large
n
, we have
3\left(n-\mathcal{O}(n^{-2})\right)t_{n-1}\leq d_{n}\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{-3})t_{n}
.

Stated differently, a uniform random realizable chirotope
\chi_{n}
on
\{1,\dots,n\}
,
is indecomposable with probability
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-3})
.

5.1.
A consequence for unlabeled chirotopes

Before proving the theorem, let us state a consequence for
unlabeled
chirotopes
.
By definition, an
unlabeled chirotope
is an equivalence class of chirotopes
for the following relation:

\chi:(X)_{3}\to\{\pm 1\}
and
\chi^{\prime}:(Y)_{3}\to\{\pm 1\}
are equivalent
if there exists a bijection
\phi:X\to Y
such that, for all
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
in
X

\chi^{\prime}(\phi(x_{1}),\phi(x_{2}),\phi(x_{3}))=\chi(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}).

Many natural notions and properties of chirotopes (being realizable, the
number of extreme points, etc.) do not depend on its labeling
and are thus well-defined on unlabeled chirotopes.
The notion of indecomposability is such a notion.

We remark that, to an unlabeled chirotope correspond several chirotopes on
\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
the number of which can vary depending on its automorphism group.
This implies that taking a uniform random realizable chirotope on
\{1,\dots,n\}
and forgetting its labeling
or taking directly a uniform random unlabeled realizable chirotope on
n
elements
lead to two different probability distributions.
However, symmetry can be controlled, and
Theorem

1.5

implies the following.

Corollary 5.1
.

Let
\bar{\chi}_{n}
be a uniform random unlabeled realizable chirotope on
n

elements. Then,
\bar{\chi}_{n}
is indecomposable with probability
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-2})
.

Proof.

Let
t^{u}_{n}
denote the number of unlabeled realizable chirotopes on
n

elements and
d^{u}_{n}
the number of decomposable ones.
Since a chirotope on
n
elements has at most
n

symmetries
[
GW23
, Theorem 1.5]
, we have

\frac{t_{n}}{n!}\leq t_{n}^{u}\leq\frac{t_{n}}{(n-1)!}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{d_{n}}{n!}\leq d_{n}^{u}\leq\frac{d_{n}}{(n-1)!}.

Theorem
1.5
asserts that
d_{n}/t_{n}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-3})
, from which we get

\frac{d_{n}^{u}}{t_{n}^{u}}\leq n\frac{d_{n}}{t_{n}}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-2}).\qed

5.2.
Some classical estimates on the number of realizable chirotopes

We start by recalling essentially known results
about the number
t_{n}
of realizable chirotopes on
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
A general reference on this topic is
[
GP93
, Section 6]
.
The first statement that we need
is the following nonasymptotic version of the case
d=2

in
[
GP93
, Theorem 6.6]
.

Proposition 5.2
.

For all
n\geq 2
, we have
t_{n}\leq e^{2n}(n-1)^{4n}
.

Proof.

We reproduce here the argument essentially given in
[
GP93
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=(p_{i,x},p_{i,y})
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, represent
an unknown set of
n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, indexed by
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
As already noticed in the proof of
Lemma

2.4
,
for
i<j<k
in
\{1,\dots,n\}
,
we have

\chi(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{k})=\sgn\left[\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}p_{i,x}&p_{j,x}&p_{k,x}\\
p_{i,y}&p_{j,y}&p_{k,y}\\
1&1&1\end{array}\right)\right].

The determinant in the right-hand side is a polynomial of degree
2
in the variables
\{p_{i,x},p_{i,y},1\leq i\leq n\}
, which
we denote
D_{i,j,k}
.
A realizable chirotope on
\{1,\dots,n\}
corresponds to a
sign pattern

of the collection
(D_{i,j,k})_{i<j<k}
of polynomials,
i.e. a choice of a sign for each
D_{i,j,k}
which can be realized
by some real specialization of the variables
\{p_{i,x},p_{i,y},1\leq i\leq n\}
.
From a result of Warren
[
War68
]
, the number of such sign patterns for general collection of polynomials
is bounded by
(\tfrac{4edm}{N})^{N}
, where
d
is the degree of the polynomials,
m
their number and
N
the number of variables.
In our case
d=2
,
m=\binom{n}{3}
and
N=2n
, and we get

t_{n}\leq\left(4e\frac{2\binom{n}{3}}{2n}\right)^{2n}

The lemma follows using the simple bound
\binom{n}{3}\leq n(n-1)^{2}/6
.
∎

The following lemma is implicit in the discussion preceeding Theorem 6.1 in
[
GP93
]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists a universal constant
K
in
(0,1/8]
such that, for all
n\geq 3
,
we have
t_{n+1}\geq K\,n^{4}\,t_{n}
.

Sketch of proof.

The number
t_{n+1}
rewrites as the sum, over all realizable
chirotopes
\chi
on
[n]
, of the number of extensions of
\chi
,
meaning the number of realizable chirotopes on
[n+1]
whose
restriction to (triples of)
[n]
is
\chi
. Fix a geometric
realization
P
of
\chi
; the number of extensions of
\chi
is
bounded from below by the number of chirotopes realized by
1
-point
extensions of
P
. This is exactly the number of cells in the
arrangement of the
\binom{n}{2}
lines through pairs of points of
P
,
which is
[
Zas97
, p. 65]

\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{2}+\binom{n}{2}+1-n\binom{n-2}{2}=\frac{n^{4}}{8}-\frac{r(n)}{8},

with
r(n)=6n^{3}-23n^{2}+26n-8\leq\frac{n^{4}}{2}
for
n\geq 3
, by a simple function analysis. The statement follows from this bound by taking
K=\frac{1}{16}
.
∎

5.3.
Some basic relations between numbers of chirotopes

Let
t^{*}_{n}
be the number of realizable chirotopes on
\{1,\dots,n\}
,
in which
n
is an extreme element.
We now relate
d_{n}
,
t_{n}
and
t_{n}^{*}
, starting with the last two.

Lemma 5.4
.

For each
n\geq 3
, we have
\frac{3}{n}t_{n}\leq t_{n}^{*}\leq\frac{4}{n}t_{n}
.

Proof.

For a chirotope
\chi
, we denote by
\mathrm{ext}(\chi)
its number of extreme elements.
By symmetry, we have:

\sum_{\chi\in{\mathcal{X}}_{n}}\mathrm{ext}(\chi)=\big|\{(\chi,i)\in{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\times[n]\colon i\text{ is extreme in }\chi\}\big|=nt_{n}^{*}.

Hence,

\frac{t_{n}^{*}}{t_{n}}=\frac{1}{nt_{n}}\sum_{\chi\in{\mathcal{X}}_{n}}\mathrm{ext}(\chi)=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{ext}(\chi)],

where
\chi
is chosen uniformly at random in

\mathcal{X}_{n}
. By
[
GW23
, Theorem 1.2]
,

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{ext}(\chi)]=4-\frac{8}{n^{2}-n+2},

which is
between
3
and
4
.
∎

Now, let us turn our attention to
d_{n}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

For each
n\geq 5
, we have

(16)

3n(t_{n-1}-d_{n-1})\leq d_{n}\leq\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n\atop n_{1},n_{2}\geq 2}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,t^{*}_{n_{1}+1}\,t^{*}_{n_{2}+1}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{*}_{n}
denote the set of realizable chirotopes on
[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{1,2,\dots,n\}
such that
n
is extreme,
and
\mathcal{D}_{n}
the set of decomposable realizable chirotopes of size
n
.
We define an application
\phi
that associates to a pair
(\chi,M)

– where
\chi\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
and
M\subseteq[n]
is a nontrivial module of
\chi
–
a tuple
\phi(\chi,M)=(\chi_{1},\chi_{2},M)
constructed as follows.
Letting
n_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|M|
and
n_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}n-n_{1}
,

•

\chi_{1}
is the chirotope obtained by replacing the elements of
[n]\setminus M
by a single element labeled
n_{1}+1
, and relabeling
M
from
1
to
n_{1}
consistently with the order of
M
;

•

\chi_{2}
is the chirotope obtained, similarly, by replacing the elements of
M
by a single element labeled
n_{2}+1
, and relabeling
[n]\setminus M
from
1
to
n_{2}
consistently.

From
Propositions

3.2
and
3.6
, it
holds that
n_{1}+1
and
n_{2}+1
are extreme elements in
\chi_{1}
and

\chi_{2}
respectively, i.e.
\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}_{n_{1}+1}
and

\chi_{2}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}_{n_{2}+1}
. We claim that
\phi
is
bijective. Indeed, take a triple
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2},M)
, with
\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}_{n_{1}+1}
,
\chi_{2}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}_{n_{2}+1}
and
M\subseteq[n]
. Let us define
\chi_{1}^{M}
as the chirotope over
M\cup\{x^{*}\}
obtained by renaming, in
\chi_{1}
, the (extreme)
element
n_{1}+1
as
x^{*}
and
[n_{1}]
as
M
, increasingly. The
chirotope
\chi_{2}^{[n]\setminus M}
is defined similarly with label
set
([n]\setminus M)\cup\{y^{*}\}
. Finally, we set
\chi=\chi_{1}^{M}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{2}^{[n]\setminus M}
and define

\Psi(\chi_{1},\chi_{2},M)=(\chi,M)
. One readily checks that
\phi

and
\Psi
are inverse of each other, showing that
\phi
is
bijective.

As a consequence, we have

d_{n}\leq|\{(\chi,M)\,:\,\chi\in\mathcal{D}_{n},\,M\text{ nontrivial module of }\chi\}|=\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n\atop n_{1},n_{2}\geq 2}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,t^{*}_{n_{1}+1}\,t^{*}_{n_{2}+1},

proving the upper bound in
Eq.

16
.

On the other hand, the chirotopes
\chi_{1}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{2}
are different for any
\chi_{1}\in{\cal X}_{3}
and
\chi_{2}\in{\cal X}_{n-1}\setminus{\cal D}_{n-1}
(this can easily be seen

e.g.
via Theorem
1.3
).
Since there are
2
chirotopes on
\{1,2,3\}
, it follows that
d_{n}\geq 2\binom{n}{2}i^{*}_{n-1}

where
i^{*}_{n-1}
is the number of indecomposable realizable chirotopes
on
[n-1]
in which
n-1
is an extreme point.
Using the same argument as in
Lemma

5.4
, we have

i^{*}_{n-1}\geq\frac{3}{n-1}(t_{n-1}-d_{n-1}),

concluding the proof of the lower bound in
Eq.

16
.
∎

Combining
Lemmas

5.4
and
5.5
, we get

(17)

d_{n}\leq 16\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n\atop n_{1},n_{2}\geq 2}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}\,t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{1}+1)\,(n_{2}+1)}.

5.4.
Bounding the sum

The goal is to prove that the upper bound in
Eq.

17
behaves as
\mathcal{O}(n^{-3}t_{n})
.
We fix some
\varepsilon
in
(0,1/2)
independent of
n

and split the sum into two parts.

(18)

\displaystyle d^{\rm small}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
2\leq\min(n_{1},n_{2})< \varepsilon n\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}\,t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{1}+1)\,(n_{2}+1)},\text{ and }

(19)

\displaystyle d^{\rm large}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}\,t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{1}+1)\,(n_{2}+1)}.

The part
d^{\rm small}_{n}
can be bounded using the classical
estimates on the number of realizable chirotopes given in
Section

5.2
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For
\varepsilon
small enough, we have
\frac{d^{\rm small}_{n}}{t_{n}}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-3})

as
n
tends to
+\infty
.

Proof.

Assuming
\varepsilon<\tfrac{1}{2}
, we write

\displaystyle n^{3}\frac{d^{\rm small}_{n}}{t_{n}}

\displaystyle=2n^{3}\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n\atop 2\leq n_{1}<\varepsilon n}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}}{n_{1}+1}\,\frac{t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{2}+1)t_{n}}.

Iterating
Lemma

5.3
, we get

\frac{t_{n_{2}+1}}{t_{n}}\leq\frac{1}{K^{n_{1}-1}(n_{2}+1)^{4}\ldots(n-1)^{4}}.

On the other hand,
Proposition

5.2
yields:

\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}}{n_{1}+1}\leq e^{2n_{1}+2}n_{1}^{4(n_{1}+1)-1}.

Bringing everything together gives us:

\displaystyle n^{3}\frac{d^{\rm small}_{n}}{t_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n\atop 2\leq n_{1}\leq\varepsilon n}\frac{n^{4}(n-1)\ldots(n_{2}+1)}{n_{1}!}\frac{e^{2n_{1}+2}n_{1}^{4(n_{1}+1)-1}}{K^{n_{1}-1}(n_{2}+1)^{5}(n_{2}+2)^{4}\ldots(n-1)^{4}}

Using that for
n
large enough,
n^{4}\leq 2(n-2)(n-1)^{3}
, and
n_{1}!\geq\left(\frac{n_{1}}{e}\right)^{n_{1}}
, we get

\displaystyle n^{3}\frac{d^{\rm small}_{n}}{t_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 4Ke^{2}\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n\atop 2\leq n_{1}\leq\varepsilon n}\left(\frac{e}{n_{1}}\right)^{n_{1}}\frac{e^{2n_{1}}n_{1}^{4(n_{1}+1)-1}}{K^{n_{1}}(n_{2}+1)^{4}(n_{2}+2)^{3}\ldots(n-3)^{3}(n-2)^{2}}\,.

When
n_{2}=n-2
, the product in the denominator
(n_{2}+1)^{4}(n_{2}+2)^{3}\ldots(n-3)^{3}(n-2)^{2}
should be interpreted as
1
(in this case, we use
n^{4}\leq 2(n-1)^{4}
).

Each of the
3(n-n_{2}-2)=3n_{1}-6
polynomial factors in the denominator are bounded from below by

n_{2}\geq(1-\varepsilon)n\geq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}n_{1}\,.

Hence, we get, for
n
large enough,

\displaystyle n^{3}\frac{d^{\rm small}_{n}}{t_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 4Ke^{2}\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right)^{6}\sum_{2\leq n_{1}\leq\varepsilon n}\left(\frac{e^{3}}{K}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)^{3}\right)^{n_{1}}\frac{n_{1}^{4(n_{1}+1)-1}}{n_{1}^{n_{1}}n_{1}^{3n_{1}-6}}

\displaystyle\leq 4Ke^{2}\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right)^{6}\sum_{n_{1}\geq 2}\left(\frac{e^{3}}{K}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)^{3}\right)^{n_{1}}n_{1}^{9}.

For
\varepsilon
small enough, we have a convergent series, showing that the right-hand size is bounded by a finite constant.
∎

We note that the above strategy
(via
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.2
)
would even fail to show that
t_{n/2+1}^{2}\leq t_{n}
, and
can therefore not be used to control
d^{\rm large}_{n}
.
In the next section, we will prove the following lemma with a completely different method.

Lemma 5.7
.

For
n\geq\frac{3}{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle t_{n}\geq\frac{3K\varepsilon^{6}}{4}\,n^{3}\,d^{\rm large}_{n}
, where
K
is the constant introduced in Lemma
5.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.5
.

Combining
Eqs.

17
,
18
,
19
,
5.6
and
5.7
,
we find that
\frac{d_{n}}{t_{n}}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-3})
, showing the upper bound in
Theorem

1.5
.
The lower bound follows from
Lemma

5.5
, using that

\frac{d_{n-1}}{t_{n-1}}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-3})
.
∎

6.
Quasi-modules and the proof of Lemma
5.7

Lemma

5.7
gives a lower bound on
t_{n}
in terms of
products
t_{n_{1}+1}t_{n_{2}+1}
where
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
. We prove it by
constructing, given two small realizable chirotopes, a larger one via a
generalized substitution operation “along a segment” (defined in
Section
6.2
). This operation is not
one-to-one, but the redundancy can be controlled using a notion of
“quasi-modules” (defined in Section
6.1
).

6.1.
Quasi-modules

Let
\chi
be a chirotope
5
5

5

Even though we are interested in realizable chirotope,
the results in
Section

6.1
are valid for abstract chirotopes in general, and thus
stated in this setting.
on a set
X
. Let
a,b\in X
and let
Y

be a subset of
X
disjoint from
\{a,b\}
. The line
ab
splits
Y

into
Y=Y_{-1}\uplus Y_{1}
, where, for
i\in\{-1,1\}
,

Y_{i}=\{y\in Y,\,\chi(a,b,y)=i\}.

We call the set
\{Y_{-1},Y_{1}\}
the
bipartition
of
Y
induced
by
a
and
b
. Note that although switching
a
and
b
exchanges

Y_{-1}
and
Y_{1}
, the induced bipartition is the same. A bipartition
is called nontrivial if
both

Y_{-1}
and
Y_{1}
are nonempty. This gives rise to a variant of the notion of modules.

Definition 6.1
.

A subset
W
of
X
is called a quasi-module of a chirotope

\chi
on
X
if every pair
\{a,b\}
of elements of
W
defines the
same bipartition of
X\setminus W
, and if this bipartition is
nontrivial (meaning that neither part is empty).

We compare the above definition to our earlier definition of module
(see
Definition

3.5
). If
M\subset X
is a module of a chirotope

\chi
, then every pair of elements of
M
defines the same
bipartition on
X\setminus M
. However, this bipartition is trivial,
so a module is not a quasi-module. Another difference is that the
definition of module is symmetric in
M
and
X\setminus M
, while
that of quasi-module is not.

An example of quasi-module is given on Figure
7
. On the other hand,
consider a chirotope
\chi
on
X
with exactly three extreme elements

\{a,b,c\}
. Each of
\{a,b\}
,
\{a,c\}
and
\{b,c\}
defines the
same bipartition
\{X\setminus\{a,b,c\},\emptyset\}
. Yet, the set

\{a,b,c\}
is not a quasi-module as it fails the nontriviality
condition.

\bullet
a
\bullet
b
\bullet
c
\bullet
d
\bullet
q
\bullet
p
\bullet
r

Figure 7.
Every line of two points in
W=\{a,b,c,d\}
defines the same bipartition of points in
\{p,q,r\}
, so
W
is a quasi-module. On the other hand,
\{a,b,c\}
is not a quasi-module of that chirotope: indeed, the line
ab
induces the bipartition
\{\{p,q\},\{d,r\}\}
,
while the line
ac
induces
\{\{p,q,d\},\{r\}\}
.

It will be useful to control the number of quasi-modules of macroscopic size of any given chirotope.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and for every
n\geq 1
such that
\varepsilon n\geq 2
,
every chirotope of size
n
has at most
n^{2}/\varepsilon
quasi-modules of
size at least
\varepsilon\,n
.

Proof.

Fix a chirotope
\chi
on a set
X
of size
n
. We will use a
double-counting argument based on an upper bound on the number of
pairs
(W,a)
, where
W
is a quasi-module and
a
a
point of
W
.

To construct such triples, we start by choosing a point
a

in
X
. We label the elements of
X\setminus\{a\}
as
x_{0}
,

x_{1}
, …,
x_{n-2}
as follows.
We choose
x_{0}\neq a
in an arbitrary way.
Then we start with the line
ax_{0}
and
rotate it in clockwise direction around
a
.
The
i
-th point encountered gets the label
x_{i}
. Note that we rotate the whole line
(infinite in both directions) and not only a half-line:
in particular,
x_{1}
can be on either side of the line
ax_{0}
.
An example is given on
Figure

8
.
The resulting labelling of
X\setminus\{a\}
depends on the chosen point
x_{0}

(namely, changing
x_{0}
results in a cyclic shift of the labels).

\bullet
a
\bullet
x_{0}
\ell_{1}
\bullet
x_{1}
\ell_{2}
\bullet
x_{2}
\ell_{3}
\bullet
x_{3}
~\ell_{4}
\bullet
x_{4}

Figure 8.
Turning the line
ax_{0}
clockwise around
a
reaches
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
and
\ell_{4}
in this order, yielding the labels
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
and
x_{4}
.

Consider a quasi-module
W
containing
a
.
Assume that
W
contains
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
for some
i<j
.
For any
h,k
such that
i<h<j<k
or
k<i<h<j
, the lines
ax_{i}
and

ax_{j}
induce different bipartitions of
\{x_{h},x_{k}\}
,
see Fig
9
.
This implies that either
x_{h}
or
x_{k}
is in
W
(possibly both).
Since this holds for any
h
and
k
such that
i<h<j<k
or
k<i<h<j
,
any quasi-module
W
containing
a
is of the form

(20)

W=\{a\}\cup\big\{x_{i},i\in I\big\},

where
I
is a
circular interval
of
\{0,1,\dots,n-2\}

(The converse is not true in general, as our
construction does not control the bipartitions of
X\setminus W

induced by lines
x_{s}x_{t}
for
x_{s}
,
x_{t}
in
W
.
In the example of
Figure

8
,
\{a,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}
is a quasi-module,
but
\{a,x_{3},x_{4}\}
is not.)
It follows that at most
n^{2}
quasi-modules
W

contain a given point
a
. There are thus at most

n^{3}
pairs
(W,a)
, where
W
is a quasi-module and
a
a
point of
W
.

\bullet
a
\bullet
x_{i}
\ell_{h}
\bullet
x_{j}
\ell_{k}

Figure 9.
The lines
ax_{i}
and
ax_{j}
divide the plane into four quadrants. These lines induce the same bipartition of
\{x_{h},x_{k}\}
if and only if these two points lie in equal or opposite quadrants. The condition
i<h<j<k
(or
k<i<h<j
) and the labelling scheme of
X\setminus\{a\}
ensures that this is not possible if
x_{h}
and
x_{k}
lie respectively on
\ell_{h}
and
\ell_{k}
.

If we denote
w_{k}
the number of quasi-modules of size
k
of the
chirotope
\chi
, then the number of pairs
(W,a)
as above is

\sum_{k=0}^{n}k\,w_{k}
. Summing up, we have

\sum_{k\geq\varepsilon n}w_{k}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}kw_{k}\leq\frac{n^{3}}{\varepsilon n}=\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon},

as announced.
∎

We conclude this section with the following lemma, which will be useful later.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\chi
be a chirotope on
X
and
W
be a quasi-module
of
\chi
. There exists a unique pair
\{w_{1},w_{2}\}\subseteq W
such
that for all
a\in X\setminus W
we have

(21)

\forall w\in W\setminus\{w_{1},w_{2}\},\quad\chi(a,w_{1},w)=\chi(a,w,w_{2})\text{ and is independent of }w.

Proof.

The statement is trivial if
W
has size two. Otherwise, fix an arbitrary point

b
in
X\setminus W
. We claim that
b
is extremal in the
restriction of
\chi
to
W\cup\{b\}
. Otherwise, by

Lemma

2.1
,
b
would be contained in a triangle

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
of points in
W
i.e. one could find
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
in

W
such that

\chi(b,w_{1},w_{2})=\chi(b,w_{2},w_{3})=\chi(b,w_{3},w_{1})=1.

By the interiority axiom, this implies
\chi(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=1
.
But from the definition of quasi-module,
there must exist a point
c\in X\setminus W

such that, for all
w,w^{\prime}
in
W
,
\chi(b,w,w^{\prime})=\chi(c,w^{\prime},w)
.
In particular,

\chi(c,w_{2},w_{1})=\chi(c,w_{3},w_{2})=\chi(c,w_{1},w_{3})=1.

Using again the interiority axiom, we get
\chi(w_{2},w_{1},w_{3})=1
,
leading to a contradiction.
Hence,
b
is indeed extremal in the restriction of
\chi
to
W\cup\{b\}
.

Therefore, by
Lemma

2.2
, the relation
w<_{b}w^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\chi(b,w,w^{\prime})=1

defines a strict total order on
W
. Call
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
the minimal and maximal
elements of
W
for this order relation.
For all
w\in W\setminus\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
, we have
w_{1}<_{b}w<_{b}w_{2}
, i.e.

\chi(b,w_{1},w)=\chi(b,w,w_{2})=1.

In particular,
Eq.

21
holds for
a=b
.
It also for other elements
a\in X\setminus W
, using the fact that
W
is a quasi-module.
This proves the existence of the pair
(w_{1},w_{2})
.

The uniqueness also follows from the fact that
<_{b}
is a total order.
Only the pair constituted by the two extrema of this total order
can satisfy
Eq.

21
for
a=b
.
∎

We refer to the two elements
\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
given by
Lemma
6.3
as the
antipodal elements
of the
quasi-module
W
.
For example, the antipodal elements of the weak module
\{a,b,c,d\}

in
Figure

7
are
b
and
d
.

6.2.
Substituting a realizable chirotope along a segment

Let
\chi
be a realizable chirotope on a set
X
and let
a\in X
be extreme
for
\chi
.
As we saw in
Lemma

2.2
, and immediately after,
we can define
a_{+}
(resp.
a_{-}
) as the unique element of
X
such that
\chi(a,a_{+},x)=1
for every
x\in X\setminus\{a,a_{+}\}

(resp. such that
\chi(a_{-},a,x)=1
for every
x\in X\setminus\{a,a_{-}\}
).
(Geometrically,
a_{-}
,
a
and
a_{+}
are consecutive points on
the convex hull of
\chi
.)
We refer to
a_{+}
(resp.
a_{-}
) as the
successor

(resp. the
predecessor
) of
a
on the convex hull of
\chi
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\chi
be a realizable chirotope on a set
X
and let
a
be an element of

X
extreme for
\chi
. Let
a_{+}
and
{a_{-}}
be, respectively, the
successor and predecessor of
a
on the convex hull of
\chi
. A

\delta
-squeezing of
(\chi,a)
is a realization
\cal P
of
\chi

such that:

•

\mathfrak{p}_{a}=(0,1)
,
\mathfrak{p}_{a_{+}}=(-1,0)
and
\mathfrak{p}_{a_{-}}=(1,0)
,

•

the
y
-coordinates of every other point of
\cal P
is in
[-\delta,\delta]
, and

•

every line going through two points of
\cal P\setminus\{\mathfrak{p}_{a}\}
has slope in
[-\delta,\delta]
.

This definition is illustrated in
Figure

10
.

Figure 10.
Two realization of the same chirotope
\chi
, the second one being a

\delta
-squeezing of
(\chi,a)
for
\delta\approx.5
(the slope of the line
bc
is close to
.5
).

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\chi
be a realizable chirotope on a set
X
and let
a
be an element of

X
extreme for
\chi
. For every
\delta>0
, the pair
(\chi,a)

admits a
\delta
-squeezing.

Proof.

Let
\cal Q
be a realization of
\chi
in which
\mathfrak{q}_{a}
is in an
unbounded cell
\Sigma
of the line arrangement
\big\{(\mathfrak{q}_{y},\mathfrak{q}_{z}),z,y\in X\setminus\{a\}\big\}
(such a realization exists by
Lemma
2.4
). Applying a translation,
a rotation and a scaling, we can further assume that
\mathfrak{q}_{a_{+}}=(-1,0)

and
\mathfrak{q}_{a_{-}}=(1,0)
while keeping
\mathfrak{q}_{a}
in an unbounded cell
\Sigma
;
note that
\mathfrak{q}_{a}
(and thus
\Sigma
) lies above the
x
-axis. Let

\vec{u}=(u_{x},u_{y})
be a direction such that
\mathfrak{q}_{a}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\vec{u}\subset\Sigma
. Note that
u_{y}
can be chosen to be positive.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta>0
, the transform

f_{\alpha,\beta}:(x,y)\mapsto(x+\alpha y,\beta y)
preserves
orientations and leaves the
x
-axis fixed. Moreover, for every
point
(x_{0},y_{0})
with
y_{0}>0
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
such
that
f_{\alpha,\frac{1}{y_{0}}}(x_{0},y_{0})=(0,1)
(namely,
\alpha=-\tfrac{x_{0}}{y_{0}}
).

Now, let
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and consider the point
\mathfrak{p}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{q}_{a}+t\vec{u}
. Let
\alpha_{t}
and
\beta_{t}
be such that

f_{\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}}(\mathfrak{p}(t))=(0,1)
and consider the point set

\cal P(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{f_{\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}}(\mathfrak{q})\colon\mathfrak{q}\in\{\mathfrak{p}(t)\}\cup\cal Q\setminus\{\mathfrak{q}_{a}\}\}

still labeled by
X
(with
\mathfrak{p}(t)
labeled by
a
and all other labels unchanged). Since
\mathfrak{p}(t)
and
\mathfrak{q}_{a}
are both in
\Sigma
,

\{\mathfrak{p}(t)\}\cup\cal Q\setminus\{\mathfrak{q}_{a}\}
has the same chirotope as

\cal Q
, that is
\chi
. Since

f_{\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}}
preserves orientations,
\cal P(t)
is also a
geometric realization of
\chi
.

It is easy to check that
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t})\to(-u_{x}/u_{y},0)
as
t\to\infty
. Consequently, the
y
-coordinate of every point of

\cal P(t)\setminus\{(0,1)\}
goes to
0
as
t\to\infty
. Also, the
slope of every line going through two points of
\cal P(t)\setminus\{(0,1)\}
goes to
0
as
t\to\infty
. Hence, for every

\delta>0
, for
t
large enough,
\cal P(t)
is a

\delta
-squeezing of
(\chi,a)
.
∎

We now use
\delta
-squeezings to construct larger realizable chirotopes out of
smaller ones.

(1)

We start with two realizable chirotopes
\chi
on
X\cup\{x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\}
and
\xi
on
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
, where
X
and
Y
are disjoint and
x_{1}^{*}x_{2}^{*}
is not an extreme edge
6
6

6

An extreme edge is an edge between two consecutive points
on the convex hull.
of
\chi
, while
y^{*}
is extreme in
\xi
.

(2)

We pick some realization
\cal P
of
\chi
with
\mathfrak{p}_{x_{1}^{*}}=(-1,0)
and
\mathfrak{p}_{x_{2}^{*}}=(1,0)
, and some
\delta
-squeezing
\cal Q
of
(\xi,y^{*})
, for some
\delta>0
small enough.

(3)

We let
\kappa
denote the chirotope on
X\cup Y
realized by the point set
({\cal P}\setminus\{\mathfrak{p}_{x_{1}^{*}},\mathfrak{p}_{x_{1}^{*}}\})\cup({\cal Q}\setminus\{{q}_{y^{*}}\})
.

For
\delta>0
small enough,
Y
is a quasi-module for
\kappa
and
(the labels of)
(-1,0)
and
(1,0)
are its antipodal elements. We
assume this is the case from now on. Let us stress that the chirotope

\kappa
depends not only on the chirotopes
\chi
and
\xi
, but on
the actual realizations
\cal P
and
\cal Q
(see
Figure
11
). We refer to the above
construction as
substituting
\xi
into
\chi
along the segment

x_{1}^{*}x_{2}^{*}
squeezing
\xi
orthogonally to
y^{*}
.

Figure 11.
Two chirotopes
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
obtained from the same substitution (substituting
\xi
into
\chi
along the segment
x_{1}^{*}x_{2}^{*}
squeezing
\xi
orthogonally to
y^{*}
), but using different realizations. Observe that
d
is not on the same side of the line
ab
in
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For any realizable chirotope
\kappa
on
Z
and any quasi-module
Y
of

\kappa
, there exist a single chirotope
\chi
on
(Z\setminus Y)\cup\{x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\}
and at most two chirotopes
\xi
on
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
such that
\kappa
can be obtained by the above
construction (up to renaming the symbols
x_{1}^{*}
,
x_{2}^{*}
and

y^{*}
).

Proof.

Suppose
\kappa
and
Y
are obtained by the above construction. Let

\{x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\}
denote the antipodal elements of
Y
, defined through
Lemma

6.3
.
Then,
\chi

must coincide with the restriction of
\kappa
to
(Z\setminus Y)\cup\{x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\}
, so it is unique (up to renaming the symbols

x_{1}^{*}
and
x_{2}^{*}
).

Since
x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\in Y
and
Y
is a quasi-module of
\kappa
, this
pair defines a nontrivial bipartition, so there exist
y^{*}_{+}
and

y^{*}_{-}
in
(Z\setminus Y)
such that
\chi(x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*},y^{*}_{-})=-1
and
\chi(x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*},y^{*}_{+})=+1
. By construction,
\xi
must
coincide with the restriction of
\kappa
to
Y\cup\{y^{*}_{-}\}
or
to
Y\cup\{y^{*}_{+}\}
. There is therefore at most two choices for
the chirotope
\xi
on
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
(up to renaming the symbol
y^{*}
).
∎

We now use this construction for counting purposes. Let
w_{n,k}

denote the number of pairs
(\chi,W)
where
\chi
is a realizable chirotope on

[n]
and
W
is a quasi-module of
\chi
of size
k
. Recall that

t_{n}^{*}
denotes the number of realizable chirotopes on
[n]
in which
n
is
extreme. Let
t_{n}^{\div}
denote the number of realizable chirotopes on
[n]
in
which
n
and
n-1
do not form an extreme edge.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any integers
n_{1}\geq 2
,
n_{2}\geq 2
, we have
w_{n_{1}+n_{2},n_{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\binom{n_{1}+n_{2}}{n_{1}}t_{n_{1}+2}^{\div}t_{n_{2}+1}^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
. Start with a triple
(I,\chi,\xi)
where
I\subset[n]
has size
n_{1}
,
\chi
is a realizable chirotope on
[n_{1}+2]
such
that
n_{1}+1
and
n_{1}+2
do not form an extreme edge, and
\xi
is a realizable
chirotope on
[n_{2}+1]
in which
n_{2}+1
is extreme. With our
notations, the number of such triples is
\binom{n_{1}+n_{2}}{n_{1}}t_{n_{1}+2}^{\div}t_{n_{2}+1}^{*}
.

Let
x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}
and
y^{*}
be formal symbols not in
[n]
. For
each such triple
(I,\chi,\xi)
, we do the following operation:

•

We let
\tilde{\chi}
denote the chirotope on
I\cup\{x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\}
obtained from
\chi
by relabeling
[n_{1}]
increasingly into
I
and mapping
n_{1}+1\mapsto x_{1}^{*}
and
n_{1}+2\mapsto x_{2}^{*}
.

•

We let
\tilde{\xi}
denote the chirotope on
([n]\setminus I)\cup\{y^{*}\}
obtained from
\xi
by relabeling
[n_{2}]
increasingly into
[n]\setminus I
and mapping
n_{2}+1\mapsto y^{*}
.

•

We substitute
\tilde{\xi}
into
\tilde{\chi}
along the segment
x_{1}^{*}x_{2}^{*}
squeezing
\tilde{\xi}
orthogonally to
y^{*}
.

The result of this operation is a pair
(\kappa,W)
where
\kappa

is a realizable chirotope on
[n]
and
W
is a quasi-module. By
Lemma
6.6
, each pair
(\kappa,W)
is the image of
at most two triples
(I,\chi,\xi)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Lemma
5.7
, that is, that for
n\geq\frac{3}{\varepsilon}
,

t_{n}\geq\frac{3K\varepsilon^{7}}{4}\,n^{3}\,d^{\rm large}_{n}\qquad\text{where}\qquad d^{\rm large}_{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\,\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}\,t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{1}+1)\,(n_{2}+1)}.

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

Lemma
5.4
gives that
t_{n}^{*}\geq\frac{3}{n}t_{n}
. Furthermore, adapting the proof of Lemma
5.4

yields

\frac{t_{n}^{\div}}{t_{n}}=\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{nee}(\chi)]

where
\mathrm{nee}(\chi)
denotes the number of pairs of points of
\chi

which are not extreme edges.
For
n\geq 5
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{nee}(\chi)]\geq\binom{n}{2}-n\geq\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{2}

and hence
t_{n}^{\div}\geq\frac{1}{2}t_{n}
. Now, by Lemmas
6.2
and
6.7
we have

\displaystyle\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon}t_{n}\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}w_{n,n_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{n_{1}}t_{n_{1}+2}^{\div}t_{n_{2}+1}^{*}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}\frac{3}{4(n_{2}+1)}\binom{n}{n_{1}}t_{n_{1}+2}t_{n_{2}+1}.

(The inequality
t_{n_{1}+2}^{\div}\geq\frac{1}{2}t_{n_{1}+2}
requires
n_{1}+2\geq 5
, which is ensured by the assumption
n\geq\frac{3}{\varepsilon}
.) By Lemma
5.3
, there
exists a universal constant
K>0
such that, for all
n_{1}\geq 2
, we
have
t_{n_{1}+2}\geq K\,(n_{1}+1)^{4}\,t_{n_{1}+1}
. Substituting into
the previous inequality and using that
n_{1}+1\geq\varepsilon n
, we obtain as desired

\displaystyle t_{n}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{n^{2}}\frac{3K}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{n_{1}}(n_{1}+1)^{5}\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{1}+1)(n_{2}+1)}

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon^{6}n^{3}\frac{3K}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
\varepsilon n\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq (1-\varepsilon)n\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{n_{1}}\frac{t_{n_{1}+1}t_{n_{2}+1}}{(n_{1}+1)(n_{2}+1)}.\qed

7.
A bijection to decompose triangulations through one bowtie product

In this section, we consider a decomposable chirotope

\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
, and relate the triangulations of

\kappa
to those of
\chi
and
\xi
. We show that each triangulation
of
\kappa
projects into triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
, and that
conversely every pair of triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
can be
obtained as projections of (possibly many) triangulations of
\kappa
.
We establish a bijection between these pre-images and
maximal
7
7

7

In what follows, inclusion-maximality is equivalent
to being of maximal cardinality.
sets of noncrossing edges between
appropriate sets.

Note:
We recall from the introduction that the notion of crossing edges
can be read on the chirotope, giving meaning to the notion that
xy
and
zt
cross in a
(possibly abstract) chirotope
\chi
, if
x
,
y
,
z
and
t
are elements of the ground set of
\chi
.
If there is no ambiguity on which chirotope
\chi
we are considering, we shall simply say that
xy
and
zt
cross.

7.1.
Statement of the bijection

In the whole section, we let
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
where

\chi
and
\xi
are chirotopes on
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
and
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
,
with
X
and
Y
disjoint,
x^{*}\notin X
being extreme in
\chi
and

y^{*}\notin Y
being extreme in
\xi
. As a preamble, note that for

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
in
X
, the pairs
x_{1}x_{2}
and
x_{3}x_{4}
cross in

\chi
if and only if they cross in
\kappa
, so we shall simply say
that
x_{1}x_{2}
and
x_{3}x_{4}
cross, without making the underlying
chirotope explicit.

Let
\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}
denote the map that sends every element of
Y
to

x^{*}
. We extend this map to sets of edges by putting, for any
triangulation
T
of
\kappa
,

\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\{\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(a),\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(b)\}\colon\{a,b\}\in T\}\setminus\{\{x^{*},x^{*}\}\}.

We define
\pi_{X\to y^{*}}
analogously. Given a set
T
of edges of

\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
, not necessarily a triangulation, and two
sets
A
and
B
, we let
T_{AB}
denote the set of edges of
T
with
one element in
A
and the other in
B
; we say that such an edge is

between
A
and
B
. As illustrated in
Figure
12
, any triangulation
T
of

\kappa
decomposes into triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
and a set

T_{XY}
of noncrossing edges. The aim of this section is to prove
that this decomposition is a bijection.

Figure 12.
A triangulation
T
of
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
, the
projection of
T
on
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
, the set
T_{XY}
, and the
projection of
T
on

Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
.

Recall that
{\cal T}_{\alpha}
denotes the set of triangulations of a
chirotope
\alpha
. Given
T\in{\cal T}_{\alpha}
and an element
x

of the ground set of
\alpha
, we write
N_{T}(x)
for the neighborhood
of
x
in
T
and
\deg_{T}(x)
for the degree of
x
in
T
. We
extend the notation
N_{T}(x)
and
\deg_{T}(x)
in the obvious manner
when
T
is a (not necessarily maximal) set of noncrossing edges.

Proposition 7.1
.

For any triangulation
T
of
\kappa
, the sets
T^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
and
T^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
are
triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
, respectively, and
T_{XY}
is a
maximal set of noncrossing edges between
N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})
and

N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})
.

Conversely, for any triangulations
T^{\prime}
of
\chi
and
T^{\prime\prime}
of
\xi

and any maximal set
H
of noncrossing edges between
N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})

and
N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})
, there exists a unique triangulation
T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\kappa}
such that
T^{\prime}=\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
,
T^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
and
H=T_{XY}
.

The proof of the bijection studies in particular the geometric
structure of the sets of noncrossing edges
T_{XY}
for any
triangulation of
\kappa
; from this structure we derive the following
combinatorial lemma that will be useful for counting
triangulations. (We use the convention
\binom{n}{-1}=\delta_{n,-1}

for any integer
n
, where
\delta
is the usual Kronecker symbol.)

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
,
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
be two nonempty subsets of
elements of
\kappa
, let
0\leq k\leq|Y^{\prime}|
, and let

z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k}
be distinct elements of
Y^{\prime}
. Then for

i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\geq 1
there are
exactly

\binom{|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k})-2}{|Y^{\prime}|-(k+1)}

maximal
sets
H
of noncrossing edges between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
and satisfying

\deg_{H}(z_{j})=i_{j}
for every
j\in[k]
.

In particular, for
k=0
, we obtain that for any nonempty subsets
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
there are exactly
\binom{|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-2}{|Y^{\prime}|-1}
maximal sets
H
of noncrossing edges in
\kappa
.

The rest of this section is devoted to proving
Lemma

7.2
and
Proposition

7.1
.

7.2.
Set of noncrossing edges between
X
and
Y

For a chirotope
\alpha
on a set
A
, we write
\mathrm{ext}(\alpha)

for the set of elements of
A
that are extreme in
\alpha
.
As we saw in
Lemma

2.2
, the relation defined by

\text{for }a,b,c\in A,\ b<_{\alpha,a}c\Leftrightarrow\alpha(a,b,c)=1

defines a strict total order on
X\setminus\{a\}
if
a
is extreme for
\alpha
.
This order allows to characterize crossings of edges between
the modules
X
and
Y
of
\kappa
.

Lemma 7.3
.

For any
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
with
y_{1}<_{\xi,y^{*}}y_{2}
, the edges
x_{1}y_{1}
and
x_{2}y_{2}
cross in

\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
if and only if
x_{1}<_{\chi,x^{*}}x_{2}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have

\chi(x^{*},x_{1},x_{2})=\kappa(x_{2},y_{2},x_{1})=-\kappa(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2})
and
similarly, we have

\xi(y^{*},y_{1},y_{2})=-\kappa(x_{2},y_{2},y_{1})=\kappa(x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
.
We also have
\xi(y^{*},y_{1},y_{2})=1
by hypothesis.
Hence if
x_{1}<_{\chi,x^{*}}x_{2}
,
then
\kappa(x_{2},y_{2},x_{1})=1=-\kappa(x_{2},y_{2},y_{1})
and

\kappa(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2})=-1=-\kappa(x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
, so that
x_{1}y_{1}
and

x_{2}y_{2}
cross in
\kappa
. Conversely, if
x_{1}y_{1}
and
x_{2}y_{2}

cross, in particular
\kappa(x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})=-\kappa(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2})
,
hence
1=\xi(y^{*},y_{1},y_{2})=-\chi(x_{1},x^{*},x_{2})=\chi(x^{*},x_{1},x_{2})
and

x_{1}<_{\chi,x^{*}}x_{2}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
,
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
be two nonempty subsets of
elements of
\kappa
. Let us write
a=|X^{\prime}|
,
b=|Y^{\prime}|
, and

y_{1}<_{\xi,y^{*}}\ldots<_{\xi,y^{*}}y_{b}
the elements of
Y^{\prime}
. The map
sending a set
H
of edges to
(\deg_{H}(y_{1}),\ldots,\deg_{H}(y_{b}))
is a bijection
between maximal sets of noncrossing edges between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}

and sequences
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{b})\in\left({\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}\right)^{b}
satisfying
d_{1}+\ldots+d_{b}=a+b-1
.

For example the set of noncrossing edges of the third picture in
Figure

12
is associated with the triple
(1,3,3)
.

Proof.

Let us write
x_{1}<_{\chi,x^{*}}\ldots<_{\chi,x^{*}}x_{a}
the elements
of
X^{\prime}
, ordered by
<_{\chi,x^{*}}
. Let
\Lambda_{a,b}
denote the
set of sequences
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{b})\in\left({\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}\right)^{b}

satisfying
d_{1}+\ldots+d_{b}=a+b-1
.

Let
H
be a maximal set of noncrossing edges between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
. For
j\in[b]
, we let
{\cal I}^{H}_{j}

denote the set of indices
i
such that
x_{i}y_{j}
is an edge of

H
.
By
Lemma

7.3
, for
j<j^{\prime}
, elements of
{\cal I}^{H}_{j^{\prime}}

are smaller than or equal to elements of
{\cal I}^{H}_{j}
.
But, using again that
H
is maximal,
we know that every element of
X^{\prime}
or
Y^{\prime}
is the endpoint of an edge,
so that each
{\cal I}^{H}_{j}
is a non-empty interval, say
[m_{j},M_{j}]
.
Moreover, consecutive
{\cal I}^{H}_{j}
must have a
common endpoint (otherwise
H
would not be maximal).
In other words, we have

1=m_{b}\leq M_{b}=m_{b-1}\leq M_{b-1}=\ldots=m_{2}\leq M_{2}=m_{1}\leq M_{1}=a.

Since
\deg_{H}(y_{j})=M_{j}-m_{j}+1=m_{j-1}-m_{j}+1
(with the convention
m_{0}=a
), it follows that
the map sending
H
to
(\deg_{H}(y_{1}),\ldots,\deg_{H}(y_{b}))

takes value in
\Lambda_{a,b}
.

Conversely, it is clear that the values
\deg_{H}(y_{j})=M_{j}-m_{j}+1

uniquely determine the tuple
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{b})
, which in turn uniquely determines

({\cal I}^{H}_{1},\cdots,{\cal I}^{H}_{b})
(recall that
M_{1}=a
and
M_{j}=m_{j-1}
for
2\leq j\leq b
),
which determines
H
.
Thus the map
H\mapsto(\deg_{H}(y_{1}),\ldots,\deg_{H}(y_{b}))
is a bijection.
∎

We can now prove
Lemma

7.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.2
.

As before, let
a=|X^{\prime}|
,
b=|Y^{\prime}|
, and let
y_{1}<_{\xi,y^{*}}\ldots<_{\xi,y^{*}}y_{b}
be the elements of
Y^{\prime}
. We write
z_{j}=y_{h_{j}}
for
some index
h_{j}
. By
Lemma

7.4
, we need to
count the number of sequences
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{b})
satisfying

d_{1}+\ldots+d_{b}=a+b-1
with
d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and

d_{h_{j}}=i_{j}
for
j\leq k
(we allow
k=0
). These sequences are
in bijection with the sequences
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{b-k})
of
b-k

positive integers summing to
a+b-1-(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k})
. They are
sometimes called compositions of
a+b-1-(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k})
into
b-k

parts, and are known to be counted by

\binom{a+b-(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k})-2}{b-k-1}
. (With the convention

\binom{n}{-1}=\delta_{n,-1}
, the formula is also correct when

k=b
.)
∎

7.3.
Proof of the bijection

The goal of this section is to prove
Proposition

7.1
. We use the
notation in the first part of its statement, namely:
T
is a
triangulation of
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
, where
\chi
and

\xi
are chirotopes on
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
and
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
, with
X

and
Y
disjoint,
x^{*}\notin X
being extreme in
\chi
and
y^{*}\notin Y
being extreme in
\xi
. We also recall that
T^{\prime}=\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
and
T^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
. Additionally we set

X^{\prime}=N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})
and
Y^{\prime}=N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})
. The set
T_{XY}
is a subset
of
T
and hence noncrossing by definition. We start by showing that
the same is true for
\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
(and symmetrically
\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
).

Lemma 7.5
.

\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
(resp.
\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
) is a set of noncrossing edges.

Proof.

Recall that whether two edges cross
or not in a chirotope
\alpha
depends only on the restriction of the chirotope

\alpha
to the four endpoints of these two edges. As
\chi
and
\kappa
coincide on
X
, no two edges of

T_{XX}
can cross in
T^{\prime}
. Furthermore, by definition of
\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}
and
\kappa
, the existence of two crossing edges
x^{*}x_{1}

and
x_{2}x_{3}
of
T^{\prime}
would lead to the existence of an
edge
y_{1}x_{1}\in T
crossing the edge
x_{2}x_{3}\in T
,
for some
y_{1}\in Y
. This is impossible since
T
is a set of
noncrossing edges in
\kappa
. This proves the lemma, the case of
\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)

being symmetrical.
∎

We still need to prove that
T_{XY}
,
\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
and
\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)

are maximal. For this, we use cardinality arguments.
As a consequence of
Lemma

7.4
, any maximal set
of noncrossing edges between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
has size
|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-1
.
In particular

(22)

|T_{XY}|\leq|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-1\,.

Besides, a classical consequence of Euler’s formula, via double counting, is
that any triangulation
\Delta
of a realizable chirotope of size
n
with

h
extreme elements has exactly

(23)

|\Delta|=3n-h-3

edges. In particular, any family of noncrossing edges of a realizable
chirotope of size
n
with
h
extreme elements has at most
3n-h-3

edges.
Eq.

23

also holds for abstract chirotopes since every
abstract chirotope can be realized by a
generalized
configuration
, that is a set of points in the plane together with
an arrangement of pseudolines, each one containing exactly two
points
[
FG17
, Theorems 5.2.6]
.

Let us come back to the setting of
Proposition

7.1
,
and let
h_{X}
(resp.
h_{Y}
) denote the number of elements of
X

(resp.
Y
) that are extreme in
\kappa
. As
T
is a triangulation of

\kappa
, we have

|T|=3(|X|+|Y|)-(h_{X}+h_{Y})-3.

By Lemma
7.5
,
T^{\prime}=\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
and
T^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
are families of noncrossing
edges of
\chi
and
\xi
, hence they have at most as many edges as triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
, i.e., using

Eq.

23
, we have

|T^{\prime}|\leq 3(|X|+1)-(h_{X}+1)-3=3|X|-h_{X}-1\qquad\text{and}\qquad|T^{\prime\prime}|\leq 3|Y|-h_{Y}-1.

By definition of
T^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime\prime}
,
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
, we further have

|T^{\prime}|=|T_{XX}|+\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})=|T_{XX}|+|X^{\prime}|,\quad|T^{\prime\prime}|=|T_{YY}|+|Y^{\prime}|\,.

Hence, recalling
Eq.

22
, we have the following inequality

|T|=|T_{XX}|+|T_{YY}|+|T_{XY}|\leq|T_{XX}|+|T_{YY}|+|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-1=|T^{\prime}|+|T^{\prime\prime}|-1.

so that

|T|\leq|T^{\prime}|+|T^{\prime\prime}|-1\leq 3(|X|+|Y|)-(h_{X}+h_{Y})-3=|T|.

This implies that every inequality we have proved so far is in fact an
equality. In particular,
T^{\prime}
(resp.
T^{\prime\prime}
) contains the maximal
possible number of noncrossing edges of
\chi
(resp.
\xi
), ensuring
that it is a triangulation of
\chi
(resp.
\xi
); and
T_{XY}

is a
maximal
set of noncrossing edges between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
.
This proves the first part of
Proposition

7.1
.

It remains to prove that for any triangulations
T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime\prime}
of,
respectively,
\chi
and
\xi
, and any maximal set
H
of noncrossing
edges between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
, there exists a unique triangulation
T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\kappa}
such that
T^{\prime}=\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
,
T^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
and
H=T_{XY}
.

Let us first argue that such a triangulation
T
is unique. Indeed, if

\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)=T
then
T_{XX}=T^{\prime}_{XX}
and the edges of
T

between
X
and
X
are determined. Similarly, the edges of
T

between
Y
and
Y
must be determined by
T_{YY}=T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}
, and the edges
between
X
and
Y
must be determined by
T_{XY}=H
.

To prove the existence of
T
, we consider the set of edges
E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}T^{\prime}_{XX}\cup T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}\,\cup\,H
. By construction,
E
satisfies

\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(E)=T^{\prime}
,
\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(E)=T^{\prime\prime}
and

E_{XY}=H
, so it suffices to prove that
E
is a
triangulation. We first argue that the size of
E
satisfies the

Eq.

23
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle|E|

\displaystyle=|T^{\prime}_{XX}|+|T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}|+|H|

\displaystyle=(|T^{\prime}|-\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*}))+(|T^{\prime\prime}|-\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*}))+(|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-1)

by
Eq.

22

\displaystyle=(|T^{\prime}|-|X^{\prime}|)+(|T^{\prime\prime}|-|Y^{\prime}|)+(|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-1)

\displaystyle=|T^{\prime}|+|T^{\prime\prime}|-1.

Let
h_{X}
(resp.
h_{Y}
) denote the number of elements of
X

(resp.
Y
) that are extreme in
\kappa
. Since
T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime\prime}
are
triangulations of respectively
\chi
and
\xi
, by

Eq.

23
,

|T^{\prime}|=3(|X|+1)-(h_{X}+1)-3=3|X|-h_{X}-1\qquad\text{and}\qquad|T^{\prime\prime}|=3|Y|-h_{Y}-1\,.

We also have
|T^{\prime}|=|T^{\prime}_{XX}|+\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})=|T^{\prime}_{XX}|+|X^{\prime}|
and

|T^{\prime\prime}|=|T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}|+\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})=|T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}|+|Y^{\prime}|
. Therefore,

|E|=3|X|-h_{X}-1+3|Y|-h_{Y}-1-1=3(|X|+|Y|)-(h_{X}+h_{Y})-3\,.

We recognize
Eq.

23
. It now remains to
argue that the edges in
E
are pairwise noncrossing. By hypothesis,
the edges in
T^{\prime}_{XX}\subseteq T^{\prime}
are pairwise noncrossing, and
similarly for the edges in
T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}
, or in
H
. An edge in
T^{\prime}_{XX}

cannot cross an edge in
T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}
as
X
and
Y
are mutually
avoiding sets in
\kappa
. Finally, an edge of
H
cannot cross in

\kappa
an edge of
T^{\prime}_{XX}
(resp.
T^{\prime\prime}_{YY}
by symmetry): suppose
by contradiction that
x_{1}y_{1}\in H
is crossing
x_{2}x_{3}\in T^{\prime}_{XX}
in

\kappa
. Then it would imply that the edges
x_{1}x^{*}
and
x_{2}x_{3}
are
two crossing edges in
T^{\prime}
, which is impossible as
T^{\prime}
is a
triangulation of
\chi
. Altogether,
E
is a triangulation of

\kappa
. This ends the proof of
Proposition

7.1
.

8.
Counting triangulations

We now explain how to count the triangulations of a chirotope
presented by a chirotope tree from information on the triangulations
of the chirotopes decorating the nodes.

8.1.
Counting the triangulations of a bowtie

Let us first consider the case of a single bowtie
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
.

Proposition

7.1
describes the
set
of triangulations of
\kappa
in terms of the
sets
of triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
. With
Lemma

7.2
, however, we can
count
the former knowing only the generating polynomials of the latters marking the degrees of the proxies
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
.

Let
P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)
and
P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)
denote the generating
polynomials of the triangulations of
\chi
and
\xi
recording,
respectively, the degrees of the elements
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
:

P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)=\sum_{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}}s^{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)=\sum_{T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}}t^{\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})}.

Here are examples with
P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)=s^{3}(s+1)

and
P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)=t^{2}(1+t+t^{2})
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
x^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
x^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
x^{*}
s^{3}
s^{4}
\chi
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
y^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
y^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
y^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
y^{*}
\xi
t^{2}
t^{4}
t^{3}

For
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
,
using Proposition
7.1
and
Lemma

7.2
,
|{\cal T}_{\kappa}|

can be expressed in terms of
P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)
and
P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)
,
as explained in the following proposition.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
where
\chi
and
\xi
are two chirotopes
on disjoint ground sets
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
and
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
,
respectively. The number of triangulations of
\kappa
is

(
6
)

|\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}\\
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\end{subarray}}\binom{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})+\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})-2}{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})-1}=\sum_{a,b\geq 2}\binom{a+b-2}{a-1}[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\ [t^{b}]P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathchoice{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}}
denote the set of triples
(T^{\prime},T^{\prime\prime},H)
such that

T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}
,
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
and
H
is a maximal set of
noncrossing edges between
X^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})
and
Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})
.
By
Lemma

7.2
(case
k=0
), for
fixed
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}
and
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
, there are

\binom{|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|-2}{|X^{\prime}|-1}

sets
H
such that
(T^{\prime},T^{\prime\prime},H)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathchoice{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}}
.
Hence, recalling that
|X^{\prime}|=\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})
and
|Y^{\prime}|=\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})
, we have

|\mathcal{T}_{\mathchoice{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}}|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}\\
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\end{subarray}}\binom{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})+\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})-2}{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})-1}.

But, from
Proposition
7.1
, we know that
|\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}|=|\mathcal{T}_{\mathchoice{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{}{}{\scriptstyle\pap}}}|
.
This proves the first equality in the proposition.
For the second equality, it suffices to group triangulations according to the
degrees of
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
(which are at least
2
in any triangulation).
∎

8.2.
Generating polynomial of the triangulations of a bowtie

We just used the bijection of Proposition
7.1
to
express
|{\cal T}_{\kappa}|
for
\kappa\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
from
P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)
and
P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)

(see
Eq.

6
). To iterate this computation
through several bowtie products, we need to determine not only
|{\cal T}_{\kappa}|
, but
P_{\kappa,z^{*}}(t)
for any extreme element
z^{*}

of
\kappa
, to be used as a proxy element in a subsequent bowtie
product.

Note that at this point,
\chi
and
\xi
do not play a
symmetric role anymore, as
z^{*}
is an element of the ground sets of
only one of them. Without loss of generality, we suppose that
z^{*}\in Y
, and we replace
P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)
by two bivariate polynomials:

Q^{\in}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\\
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\in T\end{subarray}}t^{\deg_{T}(y^{*})}u^{\deg_{T}(z^{*})}\quad\text{and}\quad Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\\
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\notin T\end{subarray}}t^{\deg_{T}(y^{*})}u^{\deg_{T}(z^{*})}.

Observe that these polynomials refine the polynomials
P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)
defined earlier, in the sense that

(24)

P_{\xi,y^{*}}(t)=Q^{\in}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,1)+Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,1).

Coming back to our previous example for
(\xi,y^{*})
, it has the following triangulations

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
z^{*}
y^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
z^{*}
y^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
z^{*}
y^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
z^{*}
y^{*}
\xi
t^{2}u^{3}
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\notin T
t^{4}u^{4}
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\in T
t^{3}u^{3}
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\notin T

.

Thus
Q^{\in}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)=t^{4}u^{4}
and

Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)=t^{2}u^{3}(1+t)
. In the following
proposition, we express
P_{\kappa,z^{*}}(u)
in terms of

Q^{\in}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)
,
Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)
and

P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)
.
8
8

8

Recalling also

Eq.

24
, the
Proposition

8.2

suggests that, by iteration, knowing the polynomials
Q
at each node
in a chirotope tree will be enough to compute the polynomial
P
for
the chirotope described by the whole tree. This is actually true only
in the special case where the tree we consider is a path (as discussed
in
Section

8.3
). Further refinements are necessary for
considering general trees, and these will be presented later, in

Proposition

9.3
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
where
\chi
and
\xi
are
chirotopes on
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
and
Y\cup\{y^{*}\}
, with
X
and
Y

disjoint,
x^{*}\notin X
and
y^{*}\notin Y
. For any
z^{*}\in Y

extreme in
\xi
we have

\displaystyle P_{\kappa,z^{*}}(u)=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b\geq 2}\tbinom{a+b-2}{a-1}[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)[t^{b}]Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)

\displaystyle+\sum_{a,b\geq 2}R_{a,b}(u)[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\ [t^{b}]Q^{\in}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u),\text{ with }R_{a,b}(u)=\sum_{i=0}^{a-1}\tbinom{a+b-i-3}{b-2}u^{i}.

Proof.

The proof relies again on the bijection of Proposition
7.1
,
and the reader is invited to refer to

Figure

12
. As before, for a
triangulation
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}
, we let
T^{\prime}=\pi_{Y\to x^{*}}(T)
,
T^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X\to y^{*}}(T)
and
T_{XY}
be the set of edges of
T

between
X
and
Y
. We let
\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}^{\in}

(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}^{\notin}
) denote the set of triangulations of

\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}
that contain (resp. do not contain) at least one edge
between
z^{*}
and an element of
X
. Note that
T
is in

\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}^{\in}
if and only if the edge
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}
belongs to

T^{\prime\prime}
.

For any
T\in\mathcal{T}^{\notin}
, we have
\deg_{T}(z^{*})=\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(z^{*})
as
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\notin T^{\prime\prime}
so that the
contribution of all triangulations of
\mathcal{T}^{\notin}
to

P_{\kappa,z^{*}}(u)
can then be computed by a straightforward
adaptation of Proposition
8.1
:

\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}^{\notin}_{\kappa}}u^{\deg_{T}(z^{*})}=\sum_{a,b\geq 2}\binom{a+b-2}{a-1}[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\ [t^{b}]Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)\,.

Now, assume that
T\in\mathcal{T}^{\in}
, so that
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\in T^{\prime\prime}
. The edges
incident to
z^{*}
in
T
are exactly the union of those incident to

z^{*}
in
T_{XY}
and those incident to
z^{*}
in
T^{\prime\prime}
, minus the
edge
\{y^{*},z^{*}\}
. Hence

\deg_{T}(z^{*})=\deg_{T_{XY}}(z^{*})+\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(z^{*})-1
. Using
Lemma

7.2
(case
k=1
) after regrouping the terms according to the
value of
i=\deg_{T_{XY}}(z^{*})
, we obtain:

\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}^{\in}_{\kappa}}u^{\deg_{T}(z^{*})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}\\
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi},\,\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\in T^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}u^{\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(z^{*})}\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})}\binom{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})+\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})-i-2}{\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})-2}u^{i-1}}_{=R_{\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*}),\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})}(u)}\,.

Grouping the terms by the values of
a=\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})\geq 2
and

b=\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y^{*})\geq 2
, we finally obtain

\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}^{\in}_{\kappa}}u^{\deg_{T}(z^{*})}=\sum_{a,b\geq 2}R_{a,b}(u)[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\ [t^{b}]Q^{\in}_{\xi,y^{*},z^{*}}(t,u)\,,

concluding the proof.
∎

8.3.
Example of application: triangulations of a family of chirotope paths

As claimed earlier, iterating
Proposition

8.2
,
it is possible to compute the polynomial
P
associated to a chirotope described by a chirotope tree that is a path, knowing the polynomials
Q
at each node in this tree.
In this section, we show an application of this fact.
Specifically, we study a family of chirotope trees,
where the underlying trees are simply paths (we call them
chirotope paths
for short),
and where the decorations are chosen among two chirotopes, symmetric of each other.
These leads, for each
k
, to
2^{k}
different chirotopes (some but not all being equivalent by symmetry) with the same number of triangulations.
This number of triangulations can be evaluated explicitly,
combining
Proposition

8.2
with the so-called
kernel method

from analytic combinatorics (see,
e.g.
,
[
BFG+02
]
).

Consider the two following chirotopes on
\{x^{*},y^{*},z,c\}
:

\chi^{(0)}=

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c
z
x^{*}
y^{*}

and

\chi^{(1)}=

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c
z
x^{*}
y^{*}

Each of
\chi^{(0)}
and
\chi^{(1)}
has a unique triangulation,
which contains the edge
\{x^{*},y^{*}\}
and where each vertex has
degree
3
. Thus,

\begin{array}[]{ccccl}P_{\chi^{(0)},y^{*}}(s)&=&P_{\chi^{(1)},y^{*}}(s)&=&s^{3},\\
Q_{\chi^{(0)},x^{*},y^{*}}^{\in}(t,u)&=&Q_{\chi^{(1)},x^{*},y^{*}}^{\in}(t,u)&=&t^{3}u^{3},\\
Q_{\chi^{(0)},x^{*},y^{*}}^{\notin}(t,u)&=&Q_{\chi^{(1)},x^{*},y^{*}}^{\notin}(t,u)&=&0.\end{array}

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\chi_{i}^{(0)}
denote a copy of
\chi^{(0)}

relabeled by
x^{*}\mapsto x_{i}^{*}
,
y^{*}\mapsto y_{i}^{*}
,
z\mapsto z_{i}
,
c\mapsto c_{i}
. We similarly define
\chi_{i}^{(1)}
to be a copy of

\chi^{(1)}
with the same relabeling. For any word
