\sigma=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\ldots\sigma_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
we put
\chi^{(\sigma)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\chi_{1}^{(\sigma_{1})}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y_{1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{2}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{2}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{2}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{2}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{2}^{(\sigma_{2})}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y_{2}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{3}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{2}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{3}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{2}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{3}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{2}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{3}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\ldots\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y_{k-1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{k-1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{k-1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{k-1}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{k}^{(\sigma_{k})}
. This expression translates into a chirotope
tree, actually a path, for example for
\chi^{(01101)}
:

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c_{1}
z_{1}
x_{1}^{*}
y_{1}^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c_{2}
z_{2}
y_{2}^{*}
x_{2}^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c_{3}
z_{3}
y_{3}^{*}
x_{3}^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c_{4}
z_{4}
x_{4}^{*}
y_{4}^{*}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
c_{5}
z_{5}
y_{5}^{*}
x_{5}^{*}

The chirotopes
\chi^{(0)}
and
\chi^{(1)}
are indecomposable and nonconvex, so
these trees are canonical and Theorem
1.3

ensures that
\chi^{(\sigma)}=\chi^{(\sigma^{\prime})}
if and only if

\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
. Moreover,
\chi^{(0)}
and
\chi^{(1)}
are
realizable so
\chi^{(\sigma)}
is realizable by
Proposition
1.1
(ii) and is the chirotope of a set
of
2k+2
elements,
k
of which are interior. It turns out that the
number of triangulations of
\chi^{(\sigma)}
depends only on the
length of
\sigma
, and there is a closed formula for this number.

Proposition 8.3
.

For every
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{k}
we have

\left|{\cal T}_{\chi^{(\sigma)}}\right|=\frac{3(2k+1){\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}}}{(2k+2)(4k+1)}-\frac{{4}^{k}{\binom{2k+2}{k+1}}}{2k+1}\sim_{k\to\infty}\frac{3-2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{16^{k}}{k^{3/2}}.

Recall that for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, the chirotope
\chi^{(\sigma)}
has
2k+2
elements. In particular, up to a multiplicative constant,
\left|{\cal T}_{\chi^{(\sigma)}}\right|
has the same asymptotics as the
2k
-th Catalan number, which is the number of triangulations of
2k+2
points in convex position.

Proof.

Let
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite binary
word, let
\tau_{k}
be its
k
th letter, and let
\tau^{[k]}
denote
its prefix of length
k
. We introduce the shorthands

P_{k}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}P_{\chi^{(\tau^{[k]})},y_{k}^{*}}(u)\quad\text{and}\quad Q(t,u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}Q^{\in}_{\chi_{k}^{(\tau_{k})},x_{k}^{*},y_{k}^{*}}(t,u)=t^{3}u^{3}

and recall that
Q^{\notin}_{\chi_{k}^{(\tau_{k})},x_{k}^{*},y_{k}^{*}}(t,u)=0
.
Since
\chi^{(\tau^{[k+1]})}=\chi^{(\tau^{[k]})}\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k+1}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k+1}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k+1}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{{}_{x_{k+1}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\chi_{k+1}^{(\tau_{k+1})}
,
we can apply
Proposition
8.2
with
z^{*}=y_{k+1}^{*}

to get

P_{k+1}(u)=\sum_{a,b\geq 2}R_{a,b}(u)\,\ [s^{a}]P_{k}(s)\,\ [t^{b}]Q(t,u)=u^{3}\sum_{a=0}^{2k+1}R_{a,3}(u)\ [s^{a}]P_{k}(s).

(Note that
a
can only range from
2
to
2k+1
as it is the degree
of a point in a triangulation of a set of
2k+2
points.) Using the identity
\displaystyle R_{a,3}(u)=\sum_{i=0}^{a-1}\binom{a-i}{1}u^{i}=\frac{u}{(1-u)^{2}}(u^{a}-1)+\frac{a}{1-u}
, we get

P_{k+1}(u)=\frac{u^{4}}{(1-u)^{2}}\underbrace{\sum_{a=0}^{2k+1}[s^{a}]P_{k}(s)\,u^{a}}_{=\,P_{k}(u)}-\frac{u^{4}}{(1-u)^{2}}\underbrace{\sum_{a=0}^{2k+1}[s^{a}]P_{k}(s)}_{=\,P_{k}(1)}+\frac{u^{3}}{(1-u)}\underbrace{\sum_{a=0}^{2k+1}a\,[s^{a}]P_{k}(s)}_{=\,P_{k}^{\prime}(1)},

which rewrites as

(25)

P_{k+1}(s)-\frac{s^{4}}{(1-s)^{2}}P_{k}(s)=-\frac{s^{4}}{(1-s)^{2}}P_{k}(1)+\frac{s^{3}}{1-s}P^{\prime}_{k}(1)\,.

Let us introduce the formal series
F(s,u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{k\geq 1}P_{k}(s)u^{k}
. Note that by definition of
P_{k}(s)
, its specialization

F(1,u)=\sum_{k\geq 1}P_{k}(1)u^{k}
is
the generating series of the sequence recording, for each
k
, the number of triangulations of
\chi^{(\tau^{[k]})}
.
Since
\chi^{(\tau^{[k]})}
is realizable, we have
P_{k}(1)\leq 30^{2k+2}

[
Sha11
]
.
Moreover, each
P_{k}
has degree at most
2k+1
and nonnegative coefficients,
so that, for
|s|\leq 2
, we have
|P_{k}(s)|\leq P_{k}(2)\leq P_{k}(1)\cdot 2^{2k+1}\leq(60)^{2k+2}
.
This implies that
F
is analytic in the two variables
s
and
u

on the domain

D=\{(s,u):|s|<2,|u|<\tfrac{1}{60^{2}}\},

which contains in particular the point
(1,0)
.
Now, multiplying
Eq.

25
by

u^{k+1}
and summing up for
k\geq 1
, we obtain the following functional
equation on
D
:

\big(F(s,u)-us^{3}\big)-\frac{s^{4}}{(1-s)^{2}}uF(s,u)=-\frac{s^{4}}{(1-s)^{2}}uF(1,u)+\frac{s^{3}}{1-s}u\partial_{s}F(1,u),

which rewrites as

(26)

\left(1-\frac{us^{4}}{(1-s)^{2}}\right)F(s,u)=us^{3}\left(1-\frac{s}{(1-s)^{2}}F(1,u)+\frac{1}{1-s}\partial_{s}F(1,u)\right),

where, as usual,
\partial_{s}F(s,u)
denotes the derivative of
F
according to
the first variable
s
.

The right-hand side of
Eq.

26
is
linear in
F(1,u)
and
\partial_{s}F(1,u)
. Let us apply the standard
kernel method
(see, e.g.,
[
BFG+02
]
): since
F(s,u)
is analytic near
(1,0)
, we can
evaluate
Eq.

26
for some
s(u)

that cancels the coefficient
1-\frac{us^{4}}{(1-s)^{2}}
, called the
kernel
, and such that
s(u)
is close to
1
when
u
is close to

0
. Two functions satisfy these conditions:

s_{1}(u)=\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+4\sqrt{u}}}{2\sqrt{u}}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{2}(u)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4\sqrt{u}}}{2\sqrt{u}}.

Substituting into
Eq.

26
yields the following linear
system:

(27)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{s_{1}(u)}{(1-s_{1}(u))^{2}}F(1,u)-\frac{1}{1-s_{1}(u)}\partial_{s}F(1,u)&=1\\
\frac{s_{2}(u)}{(1-s_{2}(u))^{2}}F(1,u)-\frac{1}{1-s_{2}(u)}\partial_{s}F(1,u)&=1\end{aligned}\right..

Solving this linear system gives us the closed form

F(1,u)=s_{1}(u)+s_{2}(u)-s_{1}(u)s_{2}(u)-1.

The rest of the proof is computational.
Since
\sqrt{1-4x}=-\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{2j-1}\binom{2j}{j}x^{j}
, we have the following expansions:

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+4x}-\sqrt{1-4x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}+1}{2j-1}\binom{2j}{j}x^{j}=2x\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{4k+1}\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}x^{2k}\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+4x}+\sqrt{1-4x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}-1}{2j-1}\binom{2j}{j}x^{j}=-2\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{4k-1}\binom{4k}{2k}x^{2k}\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-16u}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2k-1}\binom{2k}{k}4^{k}u^{k}\,.

Hence

\displaystyle s_{1}(u)+s_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{1+4\sqrt{u}}-\sqrt{1-4\sqrt{u}}}{2\sqrt{u}}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{4k+1}\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}u^{k}\,,

\displaystyle 4us_{1}(u)s_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=-1+\sqrt{1+4\sqrt{u}}+\sqrt{1-4\sqrt{u}}-\sqrt{1-16{u}}\,,

\displaystyle=-1-{2}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{4k-1}\binom{4k}{2k}u^{k}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2k-1}\binom{2k}{k}4^{k}u^{k}.

The constant terms in the last equation cancel out, so we can divide by
4u
and we obtain

F(1,u)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{4k+1}\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}u^{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{1}{4k-1}\binom{4k}{2k}u^{k-1}-\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{1}{2k-1}\binom{2k}{k}4^{k-1}u^{k-1}-1.

Grouping the terms with same degree yields
P_{k}(1)=\frac{1}{4k+1}\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{4k+3}\binom{4k+4}{2k+2}-\binom{2k+2}{k+1}\frac{4^{k}}{2k+1}
for
k\geq 1
(recalling that
P_{k}(1)
is the coefficient of
u^{k}
in
F(1,u)
, which is also the number of triangulations of
\chi^{(\tau^{[k]})}
). As
\binom{4k+4}{2k+2}/\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}=\frac{2(4k+3)}{2k+2}
, this simplifies to

P_{k}(1)=\Big(\frac{1}{4k+1}+\frac{1}{2k+2}\Big)\binom{4k+2}{2k+1}-\binom{2k+2}{k+1}\frac{4^{k}}{2k+1},

which gives the announced closed formula.
Finally, using Catalan numbers
C_{k}=\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{2k}{k}
, and their asymptotics
C_{k}=\frac{4^{k}}{k^{3/2}\sqrt{\pi}}(1+o(1))
, we can easily compute:

P_{k}(1)=\frac{3(2k+1)}{4k+1}C_{2k+1}-C_{k+1}\frac{4^{k}(k+2)}{2k+1}\sim_{k\to\infty}\frac{3-2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{16^{k}}{k^{3/2}}\,.\qed

9.
General algorithm for counting triangulations, and its complexity

Sections
7

and
8
presented a method to count the
triangulations of chirotope paths. We now generalize this method to
arbitrary chirotope trees.

9.1.
Chirotope with several marked elements and their triangulation polynomials

Let
\xi
be a chirotope with ground set
Y\cup\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
,
where
y_{1}^{*}
, …,
y_{k}^{*}
are marked
extreme
elements of
\xi
which do not belong to
Y
.
We have seen in
Section

8.2

that, with two marked elements (
k=2
), it is useful to introduce two
bivariate polynomials
Q^{\in}
and
Q^{\notin}
, separating the triangulations joining or not joining the marked elements.
For a general
k
, we shall need one
k
-variate polynomial for each possible restriction
of a triangulation to
\{y_{1}^{*},\dots,y_{k}^{*}\}
.
Such a restriction is in general a collection of noncrossing edges with extremities in
\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
,
not necessarily maximal.
We will denote the set of such configurations by
\mathcal{U}_{\{y_{1}^{*},\dots,y_{k}^{*}\}}
, or simply
\mathcal{U}_{k}
when there is no ambiguity.

Since
y_{1}^{*}
, …,
y_{k}^{*}
are
extreme
elements, the set
\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}

is in convex position, and the asymptotic behaviour of
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|
is known.

Lemma 9.1
.

For any
k
and
y_{1}^{*}
, …,
y_{k}^{*}
in convex position, we have
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|=\mathcal{O}(A^{k})
, where
A=6+4\sqrt{2}
.

Proof.

Let
U
in
\mathcal{U}_{k}
be a set of noncrossing edges with extremities in
\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
.
We first note that each edge of the convex hull can be included in
U
or not, independently of all other edges.
We therefore get that
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|=2^{k}|\mathcal{D}_{k}|
, where
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is the set of
collections of noncrossing edges with extremities in
\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
,
and containing all edges of the convex hull.
The elements of
\mathcal{D}_{k}
are called
dissections
of the
k
-gon
with vertices
\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
.
In particular, it is proved in
[
FN99
, Theorem 4]

that
|\mathcal{D}_{k}|=\mathcal{O}((3+2\sqrt{2})^{k})
. The statement follows.
∎

The collection of triangulation polynomials associated with a
chirotope
\xi
having
k
marked elements
Y^{*}=\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
is defined as follows. For a set
U\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
of
noncrossing edges, we set

Q^{U}_{\xi}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\atop T_{Y^{*}Y^{*}}=U}\prod_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}^{\deg_{T}(y_{i}^{*})},

where we recall from

Section

8
that
T_{Y^{*}Y^{*}}
is the subset of
edges of
T
with both extremities in
Y^{*}
. (To simplify notation, we
do not include the marked elements
\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
, in the
index of
Q
.) Note that the degree of each
Q^{U}_{\xi}
in a variable

y_{i}
is less than the number of elements of
\xi
, that is
|Y|+k
.
If
U
is not in
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, we use the convention
Q^{U}_{\xi}=0
.

Remark 9.2
.

We already encountered specializations of these polynomials
Q^{U}_{\xi}
in cases with few marked elements:

•

when there is no marked element, then
Q^{U}_{\xi}
is just a number, and is equal to
|\mathcal{T}_{\xi}|
(here,
U
needs to be empty);

•

when there is one marked element
y*
, then
Q^{U}_{\xi}(y)=P_{\xi,y^{*}}(y)
(here again,
U
needs to be empty);

•

and when there are two marked elements
y_{1}^{*}
and
y_{2}^{*}
,
then
Q^{U}_{\xi}(y_{1},y_{2})=Q^{\in}_{\xi,y_{1}^{*},y_{2}^{*}}(y_{1},y_{2})

(resp.
Q^{\notin}_{\xi,y_{1}^{*},y_{2}^{*}}(y_{1},y_{2})
) if
U
is the singleton containing the edge
\{y_{1}^{*},y_{2}^{*}\}
(resp. if
U
is empty).

The next step is to explain how to compute recursively
Q^{U}_{\xi}

through a bowtie operation, just like
Proposition

8.2

does for chirotopes with only two marked elements. To this end, for any set

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
of variables and integers
a,b\geq 2

satisfying
b\geq r
, we define

R_{a,b}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\})=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\geq 1}\binom{a+b-(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{r})-2}{b-(r+1)}x_{1}^{i_{1}-1}\cdots x_{r}^{i_{r}-1}.

Note that these polynomials are symmetric, justifying that we write our list of variables as a set.
We recall the convention
\binom{n}{-1}=\delta_{-1}
, leading to the following simpler formula if
b=r
:

R_{a,r}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\})=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\geq 1\atop i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=a+r-1}x_{1}^{i_{1}-1}\cdots x_{r}^{i_{r}-1}.

Other
particular cases are given by
R_{a,b}(\emptyset)=\binom{a+b-2}{b-1}

and
R_{a,b}(\{u\})
being the polynomial
R_{a,b}(u)
from

Proposition

8.2
. Just like
R_{a,b}(\emptyset)
and

R_{a,b}(\{u\})
occurred in
Proposition

8.2
to count
triangulations with zero and one edge between two marked elements,
respectively, the family
R_{a,b}(\cdot)
of multivariate polynomials
allows us to handle the general case with several marked elements.

Now, consider a second chirotope
\chi
on
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
, assume that
x^{*}
is extreme,
and build the bowtie product

\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi

on ground set
X\cup Y\cup\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k-1}^{*}\}
. We want to compute
the associated collection of triangulation polynomials

Q^{U}_{\kappa}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1})=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}\atop T_{\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k-1}^{*}\}\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k-1}^{*}\}}=U}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}y_{i}^{\deg_{T}(y_{i}^{*})},

for
U\in\mathcal{U}_{k-1}
. The bijection of

Proposition

7.1
between the triangulations of
\kappa
and those of

\chi
and
\xi
leads to the following formula, which generalizes
Proposition
8.2
.

Proposition 9.3
.

Let
\chi
,
\xi
and
\kappa=\chi\mathchoice{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}{\sideset{{}_{x^{*}}}{{}_{y_{k}^{*}}}{\scriptstyle\pap}}\xi
be as
above. For any
U
in
\mathcal{U}_{k-1}
, we have

(28)

\hskip-2.84526ptQ^{U}_{\kappa}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1})=\sum_{S\subseteq[k-1]\atop a,b\geq 2}\Big([s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\Big)\cdot R_{a,b}(\{y_{i},i\in S\}\cdot\Big([y_{k}^{b}]Q^{U\cup Y^{*}(S,k)}_{\xi}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{k})\Big),

where
Y^{*}(S,k)=\{\{y^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{k}\},i\in S\}
.

Proof.

We write
Y^{*}_{k-1}=\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k-1}^{*}\}
,
Y^{*}_{k}=\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
,
and
\tilde{Y}=Y\cup Y^{*}_{k-1}
, for short.
For
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}
a triangulation of
\kappa
, we let

T^{\prime}=\pi_{\tilde{Y}\to x^{*}}(T)
and
T^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X\to y_{k}^{*}}(T)
, as usual.
We also recall that
T_{X\tilde{Y}}
denotes the set of edges in
T
joining

X
to
\tilde{Y}
. We recall from Proposition
7.1

that the map
T\mapsto(T^{\prime},T^{\prime\prime},T_{X\tilde{Y}})
is a bijection.

Fix
U
in
\mathcal{U}_{k-1}
. We note that
T
and
T^{\prime\prime}
coincide on
Y_{k-1}^{*}
.
Thus, the restriction of
T
to
Y_{k-1}^{*}
is
U
if and only if that of
T^{\prime\prime}
is
U
as well.
This implies that the restriction of
T^{\prime\prime}
to
Y_{k}^{*}

is of the form
U\cup Y^{*}(S,k)
for some
S\subseteq[k-1]
.
To shorten notation, for a given
S
, we let
\mathcal{T}^{U,S}_{\xi}
be the set of triangulations
T^{\prime\prime}
of
\xi

whose restriction to
Y_{k}^{*}
is equal to
U\cup Y^{*}(S,k)
.

With the same argument as in
Proposition

8.2
,
we see that the degree of
y_{i}^{*}
in
T
are computed as follows:

•

if
i
is not in
S
, i.e. if
y_{i}^{*}
is not connected to
y_{k}^{*}
in
T^{\prime\prime}
,
then

\deg_{T}(y_{i}^{*})=\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{i}^{*}).

•

if
i
is in
S
, i.e. if
y_{i}^{*}
is connected to
y_{k}^{*}
in
T^{\prime\prime}
, then

\deg_{T}(y_{i}^{*})=\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{i}^{*})+\deg_{T_{X\tilde{Y}}}(y_{i}^{*})-1.

Hence we have:

(29)

Q^{U}_{\kappa}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1})=\sum_{S\subseteq[k-1]}\,\sum_{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\chi}}\,\sum_{T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{U,S}_{\xi}}\,\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}y_{i}^{\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{i}^{*})}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{T_{X\tilde{Y}}}\prod_{i\in S}y_{i}^{\deg_{T_{X\tilde{Y}}}(y_{i}^{*})-1}\right).

In the last sum,
T_{X\tilde{Y}}
runs over maximal sets of noncrossing edges between

N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})
and
N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})
(see the statement of
Proposition

7.1
). In particular,
even if it is not explicit in the notation, this last sum depends on
T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime\prime}
.
Using
Lemma

7.2
, we see that

\sum_{T_{X\tilde{Y}}}\prod_{i\in S}y_{i}^{\deg_{T_{X\tilde{Y}}}(y_{i}^{*})-1}=\sum_{(d_{i})_{i\in S}\atop d_{i}\geq 1}\left(\prod_{i\in S}y_{i}^{d_{i}-1}\right)\binom{|N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})|+|N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})|-\big(\sum_{i\in S}d_{i}\big)-2}{|N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})|-(|S|+1)}\\
=R_{|N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})|,|N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})|}(\{y_{i},i\in S\}).

We split the sums in
Eq.

29
, depending on the values
of the parameters
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|N_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})|=\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})
and
b\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|N_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})|=\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})
.
For given
a,b\geq 2
, there are
[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)
triangulations
T^{\prime}
with
\deg_{T^{\prime}}(x^{*})=a

and we have

\sum_{T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{U,S}_{\xi}\atop\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{k}^{*})=b}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}y_{i}^{\deg_{T^{\prime\prime}}(y_{i}^{*})}\right)=[y_{k}^{b}]Q^{U\cup Y^{*}(S,k)}_{\xi}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k}).

This implies

Q^{U}_{\kappa}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1})=\sum_{S\subseteq[k-1]}\sum_{a,b\geq 2}\big([s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\big)\big([y_{k}^{b}]Q^{U\cup Y^{*}(S,k)}_{\xi}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\big)\,R_{a,b}(\{y_{i},i\in S\}),

as announced.∎

9.2.
A general algorithm

We now explain how to use
Proposition

9.3
to count the
triangulations of a chirotope presented by a chirotope tree
T
. We
proceed as follows.

(1)

For each node
v
, we denote by
\chi_{v}
the chirotope
decorating the node
v
and by
Y_{v}
its set of proxy elements, and
we compute the associated collection of triangulation polynomials

\{Q^{U}_{\chi_{v}}\colon U\in\mathcal{U}_{Y_{v}}\}
. These polynomials
count triangulations of
\chi_{v}
, keeping track of the degree of
each elements of
Y_{v}
in the triangulation, and of the restriction
of the triangulation to
Y_{v}
. These polynomials can be computed in
parallel for all nodes
v
, using for each one a modified version of
the Alvarez-Seidel algorithm
[
AS13
]
.

(2)

As long as the chirotope tree is not reduced to a single node,
we pick an arbitrary leaf and perform an edge contraction (as
defined in
Section

4.2
) to merge it it with its
parent. We compute the collection of triangulation polynomials to be
associated to the merged node using
Proposition

9.3
: we
take
\chi
to be the merged leaf, and use a single marked element

x^{*}
. In that case, as noted by
Remark

9.2
,
P_{\xi,x^{*}}(u)=Q^{U}_{\xi}(u)
, with
U
necessarily empty. For further details, see
the proof of
Proposition

9.4
below.

Each application of Step (2) yields a strictly smaller chirotope tree
that, by
Proposition

4.7
, represents the same
chirotope, and for which we know the collection of triangulation
polynomials of every node. Hence Step (2) can indeed be repeated
until the tree is reduced to a single node. When this is the case,
there are no more marked elements, so the associated collection of
triangulation polynomials is actually a single integer, equal to the
number of triangulations of
\chi_{T}
.

The complexity of this algorithm is given in the following
proposition. We recall from the introduction (see also

Lemma

9.1
) that
A
stands for the constant
6+4\sqrt{2}\approx 11.66
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
T
be a chirotope tree.

(i)

If a node
v
of
T
is decorated with a chirotope

\chi_{v}
with
n_{v}
elements, including a set
Y_{v}
of
k

proxies, then
\{Q_{\chi_{v}}^{U}\colon U\in\mathcal{U}_{Y_{v}}\}
can
be computed from
\chi_{v}
in time
O\left(A^{k}2^{n_{v}}n_{v}^{k+2}\right)
.

(ii)

If
T
has
m
edges, each of its node has degree at most
k
, and the ground set of

\chi_{T}
has
n
elements, then the above algorithm computes the number of triangulations of

\chi_{T}
from the polynomials
\{Q_{\chi_{v}}^{U}\colon v\text{ a node of }T,U\in\mathcal{U}_{Y_{v}}\}
in time

O\left(A^{k}n^{2k}m\right)
.

Proof.

Statement (i) follows from a simple modification of the
Alvarez-Seidel algorithm
[
AS13
]
so as to compute
the degrees of marked vertices and keep track of the presence of
edges between them through the aggregation.

To prove Statement (ii), let us now analyze one iteration of Step
(2). Consider a leaf
\ell
of the chirotope tree
T
, and denote by

v
its parent node. We let
\xi
denote the chirotope decorating

v
and denote by
Y\cup\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k}^{*}\}
its ground set,
with
k\geq 2
(where, as usual, the starred elements indicate the
proxies). Similarly, we let
\chi
denote the chirotope decorating
the leaf
\ell
and
X\cup\{x^{*}\}
its ground set (a leaf has only
one proxy element, denoted
x^{*}
here).

Let
\mathcal{U}_{k}=\mathcal{U}_{\{y_{1}^{*},\dots,y_{k}^{*}\}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{k-1}=\mathcal{U}_{\{y_{1}^{*},\dots,y_{k-1}^{*}\}}
. For each
U\in\mathcal{U}_{k-1}
, we
initialize
{\tilde{Q}}^{U}_{\xi}=0
. Now, for every
\bar{U}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
, we denote
p(\bar{U})
the restriction of
\bar{U}
to

\{y_{1}^{*},\ldots,y_{k-1}^{*}\}
and
S_{\bar{U}}=\{i\in[k-1],y_{i}^{*}y_{k}^{*}\in\bar{U}\}
. Then we compute

(30)

\sum_{a,b\geq 2}\Big([s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)\Big)\cdot R_{a,b}(\{y_{i},i\in S_{\bar{U}}\})\cdot\Big([y_{k}^{b}]Q^{\bar{U}}_{\xi}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{k})\Big)

and add it to
{\tilde{Q}}^{p(\bar{U})}_{\xi}
.
By
Proposition

9.3
, when all
\bar{U}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
have been treated,
we have
{\tilde{Q}}^{U}_{\xi}=Q^{U}_{\xi}
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}_{k-1}
. This proves the correctness of our algorithm.

Let us analyze the complexity of this second step. We first
consider, for given
\bar{U}
and
a,b
, the multiplication in

Eq.

30
. The first factor
[s^{a}]P_{\chi,x^{*}}(s)
is
a scalar, while the two other polynomials are
(k-1)
-variate and
have degree less than
n
in each variable (in general the degree of
a polynomial
Q^{V}_{\alpha}
in a variable
y_{i}
is less than the
number of elements, including marked ones, in
\alpha
). Therefore
the last two polynomials have each at most
n^{k-1}
monomials, and
the multiplication has complexity
\mathcal{O}(n^{2(k-1)})
(in the Real-RAM
model
9
9

9

Recall that, in the Real-RAM model, adding or
multiplying any two scalars takes constant time. For a more
realistic complexity analysis, we note that the bit complexity of
every number arising in the computation is
\mathcal{O}(n)
, as each such
number is bounded from above by the number of triangulations, which
is
\mathcal{O}(30^{n})
.
, using a brute-force multiplication algorithm).
Since we only need to consider the case
a,b\leq n
(further terms
are equal to 0), and since there are
\mathcal{O}(A^{k})
sets
\bar{U}
in

\mathcal{U}_{k}
(see
Lemma

9.1
), this step has complexity

\mathcal{O}(n^{2k}A^{k})
.

Altogether, one iteration of Step (2), that is the merge of a leaf
of a chirotope tree with its parent, and the update of the
associated collection of polynomials, can be done in time

\mathcal{O}(n^{2k}A^{k})
. Notice that the strict upper bound of
n
on the
partial degree of these polynomials remains true, as it follows from
the fact that in any chirotope tree representing a chirotope of
size
n
, every node has at most
n
elements. As
T
has
m

edges, Step (2) needs to be iterated
m
times until the tree is
reduced to a single vertex. This completes the proof of
Proposition
9.4
.
∎

Figure 13.
A chirotope tree with 36 nodes, each decorated with a chirotope
of size
9
, adding up to
254
elements. The numbers next to each
node indicate the identifier of the decorating chirotope in the
order type database
[
AAK02
]
, as well as the label, in this database entry, of the proxy shown in red in the figure.

9.3.
A proof-of-concept implementation

We implemented the method described in the previous section (both the
modification of the Alvarez-Seidel algorithm and the recursive
counting behind Proposition
1.4
, restated above as
Proposition

9.4
) for the
special case of trees
T
where

•

every node has degree at most
3
,

•

every node of degree
3
is decorated by a chirotope with only

3
extreme elements, which are its proxies, and

•

every node of degree
2
has its two proxies consecutive on the
convex hull.

These conditions ensure that in any triangulation of a chirotope
decorating a node of
T
, there is always an edge between any two
proxies, i.e. we only have one non-zero polynomial attached to each
node. We propose this as a proof of concept: we made little effort in
optimizing or benchmarking it as either is beyond the scope of this
paper.

9.3.1.
Repository

Our implementation runs within Sagemath
to take advantage of basic
functions for the manipulation of multivariate polynomials, but is
mostly basic python. The code in Sagemath can be found on the
repository

chirotope_tree
. The
example of Figure
13
can be obtained by running the
command
sage -python example.py
, which prints two numbers,
first the number of triangulations of the example, and the total time (in seconds) spent on computing it (ignoring the initial time to launch
sage).

9.3.2.
An example

Our code computes in a few seconds on a standard laptop that the
number of triangulations of the chirotope of size
254
presented by
the tree of Figure
13
